#6957. Ransomware Attacks Move Beyond Enterprise Systems to Theaten Employees' Families

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

In a recent announcement, the company highlighted findings from its 2026 Ransomware Risk Report, which examines AI-related attack tactics, personal intimidation, and recovery preparedness. Censuswide surveyed 1,200 information technology and security professionals across nine countries and eight industry sectors in the first half of 2026.

The sample has an important limit: This year's study included only organizations where at least one ransomware attack caused business disruption. The results describe those victims' experiences, rather than the likelihood that an organization will suffer an attack. The report says that sampling approach changed from previous studies.

Semperis sells identity security, recovery and crisis management products.

The report says 84% of respondents experienced attacks that exploited AI in some way. Its breakdown distinguishes AI-based attacks, reported by 40%, from attacks that manipulated victims' own AI agents or systems, reported by 44%. That distinction matters: The broader figure covers both attackers using AI and attackers exploiting AI deployed inside an organization.

Defensive use of AI also produced mixed responses. Nearly half of respondents, 49%, said they successfully used AI for detection or response, while 23% said their AI tools failed to detect or respond successfully. Those are survey responses about victimized organizations, rather than a controlled comparison of security products.

Chris Inglis, former U.S. national cyber director and a Semperis strategic adviser, described the difficulty of recognizing AI's involvement. "Many attacks are not perceived as being aided and abetted by AI because they're just too slick, too sophisticated," he said in the report.

Data erasure, destruction, or release remained the most frequently reported threat, at 56%. Blackmail, extortion or public shaming followed at 41%, with system lockouts or operational sabotage at 40%. The report also recorded psychological intimidation at 23% and physical threats at 17%.

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

#6951. IDC Report Finds Significant Gap Between AI Spending and Measurable Financial Returns

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

The research firm's 2026 Worldwide AI Economics and Cost of Ownership Survey found that 69% reported such a gap, while only 15% had achieved an AI return on investment exceeding 100%.

In a separate May 2026 survey, 73% of respondents identified excessive AI costs as a significant risk.

IDC highlighted the findings in an October 20 analysis examining what enterprise customers expect from AI technology vendors.

Enterprise customers are placing greater emphasis on measurable business outcomes when evaluating AI products, according to IDC.

The firm identified three priorities for vendors: demonstrating financial value, addressing governance and security requirements, and building support beyond IT departments.

The analysis also highlighted the gap between initial agentic AI deployments and broader adoption. Although 80% of organizations have started deploying agentic AI, only 17% are using it across the enterprise.

IDC said evaluating AI investments can be difficult when the technology changes how employees work and how business processes operate.

The firm expects AI infrastructure capacity to catch up with demand around 2029, potentially easing costs. Until then, vendors face pressure to demonstrate the long-term financial value of their products.

AI funding decisions involve a broad range of executives. IDC's September 2026 C-Suite Tech Survey found that CIOs and CTOs were involved in 63% of those decisions, business-function executives in 62%, and chief financial officers in 61%.

A separate IDC survey found that 42% of CEOs planned to hire a chief AI officer within the next 12 months.

Buyers also reported concerns about the products and terms vendors offer. Limited integration with existing systems, insufficient model and data transparency, and concerns about vendor lock-in were each cited by 34% of respondents.

Overpromising and underdelivering, along with unclear pricing models, were each cited by 31%.

IDC said vendors need to substantiate financial returns and address security and governance requirements while working with decision-makers across IT, finance, and other business departments.

For more information, go to the IDC blog.

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

#6945. Report: Global Ransomware Attacks Rise 23% in Q3

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

The company's Cyber Threat Intelligence Report Review of Q3 2026 recorded 2,865 ransomware victim claims, up from 2,325 in the previous quarter.

Despite the quarterly increase, attacks declined 26% month over month in September to 821. North America recorded the most attacks that month with 390, followed by Europe with 211.

The industrial sector accounted for 29% of quarterly attacks, while Qilin remained the most active ransomware group for the sixth consecutive quarter, recording 365 victims. The Gentlemen followed with 356, and INC Ransom ranked third with 109.

The report highlighted a July cyber attack against a small UK power generation facility that forced operations to stop for approximately four days.

Although the incident did not affect the national electricity grid, it demonstrated how cyber attacks can disrupt physical infrastructure.

The security researchers said the incident was consistent with techniques previously associated with Iranian-affiliated campaigns, including attacks against internet-exposed industrial controllers.

However, the UK government has not formally attributed the attack, and the techniques used remain unconfirmed.

The report also identified growing security challenges as organizations connect traditional IT networks with operational technology (OT) systems for remote maintenance and centralized management.

The firm recommended network segmentation, stronger privileged-access controls, and continuous monitoring of OT environments.

The research found that more than 40 vulnerabilities affecting enterprise applications, VPN appliances, e-mail gateways, and management platforms were added to CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog during the quarter.

The report examined Gunra, a ransomware-as-a-service operation that has targeted 53 victims across multiple industries as of Q3.

According to the cybersecurity company, Gunra exploited vulnerabilities CVE-2024-55591 and CVE-2025-24472 in internet-facing Fortinet devices to gain access to corporate networks.

The group also used stolen credentials, session hijacking, and administrative tools to move between systems before deploying ransomware.

Other emerging ransomware groups included Deadlock and Storm, with the latter claiming 78 victims by the end of September.

Researchers also observed initial access brokers advertising compromised VPN, Remote Desktop Protocol, and SSH access, alongside access to cloud identities, source code repositories, and security management platforms.

The quarterly review also highlighted BraZetsu, an AI-enhanced Windows malware framework that evolved into a specialized initial access toolkit.

According to the report, BraZetsu uses AI models to automate victim profiling, analyze compromised environments, and identify valuable corporate systems.

The framework searches for enterprise software, financial data, digital certificates, and cloud credentials, helping operators identify compromised access that can be sold to other cyber criminals.

The cybersecurity firm also identified risks involving legitimate AI agents, including prompt injection, excessive permissions, and unintended access to external systems.

The report cited incidents involving U.S. and Australian government systems as examples of agents accessing resources beyond their intended scope.

Researchers warned that these incidents do not necessarily involve malicious intent but can create consequences resembling conventional cyber intrusions.

For the full report, visit the NCC Group site.

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

#6940. Microsoft Expands Protections and Controls for Sovereign Cloud

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

In a recent announcement, Douglas Phillips, president and chief technology officer of Microsoft Specialized Clouds, outlined updates spanning public cloud, private cloud, and partner environments. The changes target governments, regulated industries, and organizations that need tighter control over sensitive workloads without abandoning cloud-based AI capabilities.

"Digital sovereignty should give organizations greater control and security over the technology they depend on, while continuing to innovate," Phillips wrote.

One of the biggest additions is Microsoft's Sovereign Control Panel, which will enter public preview at Microsoft Ignite. Available through the Azure portal, the tool will give IT administrators one place to manage data sovereignty settings, spot compliance issues, and identify potential risks that could disrupt operations.

Microsoft is also tightening controls over who can access sensitive systems. Its Data Guardian feature will require oversight by personnel based in Europe when Microsoft engineers access certain production systems in the European Union and European Free Trade Association. Another tool, Production Touch Ledger, will keep tamper-resistant records of that access, giving organizations a clearer picture of who accessed their systems and what they did.

Microsoft is also giving customers more control over encryption. External key management with Azure Managed HSM is now generally available, letting organizations keep their main encryption keys on hardware they own or manage outside Microsoft's cloud.

The company is also expanding its Sovereign Private Cloud offerings, giving organizations more options to run systems with or without an internet connection. New capabilities include GitHub Enterprise Local, GitHub Copilot with locally hosted AI models, and additional data services running on Azure Local.

The push for greater operational control comes as Microsoft is also reconsidering how enterprises should secure increasingly autonomous AI systems.

As reported by Redmond magazine, Microsoft is expanding Project Perception to address risks posed by what the company calls "agentic insiders," AI agents capable of accessing sensitive information and performing actions without constant human supervision.

That initiative introduces plans for Guardian Agents, specialized security agents designed to monitor other AI systems, evaluate their behavior and intervene when their actions become risky.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," said Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security.

Project Perception is about limiting what AI agents can do. Microsoft's sovereign cloud expansion tackles a different set of questions: where those systems run, who gets to administer them, and how the data stays protected.

Taken together, the two efforts point to a shift in Microsoft's enterprise AI strategy. The company is putting more weight on oversight, not just automation.

Phillips said AI is making these questions more pressing as organizations roll out intelligent systems across sensitive business processes and critical infrastructure.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#6938. This Designer-Approved Curtain Trick Will Instantly Elevate Your Bedroom

Your window treatments should serve up both form and function.

Window treatments are often an afterthought, but your choice of drapery can make or break your entire design. Our 2026 Whole Home in Charleston is a master class in innovative design, and our designers took every opportunity to showcase their skills and style, down to the very last detail—including some gorgeous examples of transformative drapery.

Though often seen as a luxury, window treatments are a home necessity. Blinds, drapes, and shades don’t just line our windowpanes; they block drafts, light, and real-world clamor from entering your homes, practically on demand. While some choose to keep it simple, designers know your sill is uncharted territory for design potential. While it might be tempting to reach for your typical verticals, it’s worth making a statement. That said, good drapes can be expensive, so if you’re hoping to embrace your window treatment’s potential, you’ll have to budget for it.

In our patternmaxxed guest bedroom, designer Laura Lubin, founder of the Nashville-based design firm Ellerslie Interiors, used Stroheim drapes to add subtle color to her muted palette and elevate the chic TWOPAGES Roman shades just beyond. Then she took her design a step further: Instead of relying on pleating for texture, Lubin added a leafy trim and Fabricut bullion fringe to the edge of her panels, framing the bold blue baseboard and adding another note of texture to the room’s design.

Texture is meant to act as a bridge in design schemes, connecting elements and feeding both our visual and tactile appetites. In addition to her customized drapes, Lubin used grid-patterned wallpaper, natural wood grain, and plaid textiles to elevate the visual palette.

If you’re looking for a quick bedroom upgrade with big impact, it might be time to take a closer look at your drapes. After all, your window treatments are meant to block out the world, not your style.

#6935. Cisco Intros Privileged Infrastructure Access for People and AI Agents

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

In a recent blog post, Cisco's Matt Caulfield described a system that uses cryptographic identity to connect an administrator or agent to the infrastructure needed for a particular task. Access is governed by short-lived privileges, with session recording providing a record of the work performed.

"If there is no work being done, no privilege exists," Caulfield wrote. Cisco positions the offering as a way to bring identity controls directly into infrastructure operations while reducing dependence on static credentials, shared secrets, and broad access.

The announcement follows Cisco's previously announced Teleport partnership and its Infrastructure Identity concept, which connects the identity of a person or machine with the resources it can access, the actions it performs, and the permissions governing those actions.

Caulfield described a gap between the controls organizations apply to workforce applications and those used to administer network infrastructure. Companies have invested in single sign-on, multifactor authentication, and risk-based controls for application access, he wrote, but those protections often do not follow an engineer logging into a router or switch.

Instead, infrastructure access may depend on long-lived credentials, shared accounts, or separate policy systems. According to Cisco, that separation makes it harder to enforce least privilege and establish which person or system performed a particular action.

The company illustrates the problem with an administrator updating hundreds of switches during a maintenance window. If some updates fail, the administrator needs to troubleshoot before the window closes. The permissions available to handle that work, however, may cover more infrastructure and remain active longer than the task requires.

Traditional privileged access management can reduce risk through credential vaulting and rotation, Caulfield wrote. But he argued that operational friction can undermine those controls when teams create exceptions to complete urgent work. Those exceptions can leave credentials and privileges usable after the immediate need has passed.

Cisco's stated objective is to give infrastructure teams a consistent route to the devices they manage while providing security teams with policy enforcement, temporary permissions, and a record of activity. The company describes operational convenience as part of the design, rather than treating access governance solely as a credential-management problem.

Privileged Infrastructure Access makes access an on-demand action, according to the announcement. Cryptographic identity establishes the actor, and the resulting connection provides only the privileges needed to complete the task. Those privileges expire when the task concludes.

For customers using Cisco Identity Services Engine (ISE) and Terminal Access Controller Access-Control System Plus (TACACS+), the offering can draw on existing policy and device context to govern access to Cisco routers and switches. Cisco says customers can extend the same access model to other infrastructure, including servers, Kubernetes clusters, and cloud resources.

Anthropic announced it is introducing machine-readable watermarks to text generated by supported Claude models as it moves to comply with European Union AI transparency rules.

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

Sixteen companies were selected as winners for their product achievements.

Microsoft has published a draft code of conduct that lays out how its homegrown AI models should behave, with human control taking priority over model capability, autonomy, and even task completion.

#6933. 8 Popular Home Items From 20 Years Ago That Are Making a Surprising Comeback

They may be a blast from the past, but they’re about to be very now.

Trends come and go, but they almost always come back again. If there’s one thing we know about interior design, it’s that what feels old and outdated manages to become fresh and cool again in a few years’ time. Skeptics can easily be proved wrong with the resurgence of sunken living rooms, glass block walls, and even cushy sofas plucked right out of a Nora Ephron movie. And, as younger generations continue to be intrigued with the 1990s and early 2000s, they are turning to the era’s interiors for inspiration.

Well, sort of, as Maggie Vollrath of Provision Studios points out. “When we look back at certain interiors from the 2000s, there was such a commitment to achieving a particular look that every element in the room was chosen to reinforce it,” the Georgia designer says. “A home should feel like a collection of things you genuinely love, not as though you’ve walked onto a movie set designed to represent a particular style.”

When you cherry-pick trends and design details from the past, you’re able to create a home that honors the past without appearing stuck in the past. “The goal isn’t to make a home look like it belongs to another decade,” Vollrath says. “You want to bring back the beauty and character of those decorative details while creating something that feels authentic to both the house and its owners.”

Below, designers share trends from the past that the next generation of design lovers adore.

For older generations, a swivel chair might conjure memories of sitting in your family’s den, but designers promise these movin’, groovin’ seats have been revived for the 21st century. “Swivel barrel chairs defined early-2000s transitional interiors, usually in a bright flat polyester-blend upholstery,” explains Hallie Malitsky, principal designer of Hallie Rose Design in Brooklyn. “The version coming back today is a different experience entirely.”

Today’s iterations, she insists, are subtler. Cheap polyester has yielded to rich, tactile fabrics like wool, linen, and velvet. For a timely twist, she also encourages trading the fire engine red and butter yellow popular in the 2000s for burgundy and ochre. “Surround it with natural materials like wood, linen, and stone, and [your chair] will stop being the loudest thing in the room,” she recommends.

When Malitsky thinks of early aughts design, she immediately envisions the gigantic walnut media center that plagued many of her friends’ family rooms. “Dark wood cabinetry is a notorious culprit of bad 2000s design, but the wood wasn’t the issue,” she says. “It was the fact that there was so much of it.”

Now that dark wood tones are making a comeback, Malitsky says to use them sparingly. Two to three walnut or ebony pieces per room will make enough of a statement. And, no: Unlike the living rooms of yore, your furniture doesn’t have to match.

“What I love about their return is that we’re incorporating these pieces in a much more collected way,” shares Vollrath. “Rather than purchasing an entire matching furniture collection, we’re mixing periods and bringing in individual pieces that have character and history.”

The early aughts had lots of elaborate cabinetry, replete with corbels, substantial cornices, and beautifully detailed range hoods. As everyone—yes, even younger generations—continue to favor more traditional interiors, we’re seeing a renewed interest in ornamented cabinetry.

“There’s something really special about a kitchen that feels as though it could have been there for generations, or even reminds you of your grandmother’s kitchen,” Vollrath says. “Those decorative flourishes bring a sense of history, craftsmanship, and permanence that can be difficult to achieve with a more streamlined approach.”

But why stop there when you can add character to other rooms of the house?

“We’re seeing a broader interest in architectural detailing throughout the home, from wainscoting and wall paneling to ceiling beams and substantial moldings,” she adds. “Homeowners are becoming more interested in understanding these elements, considering them during renovations, and making them a priority in new construction.”

If the rise of analog interiors proves anything, it’s that even the best chatbot can’t beat excellent design. Instead of having a home covered in screens and smart systems, many are favoring more primitive setups that harken back to dial-up and CD-ROMs. For example, Fargo-based designer Lindsey Christie has been noticing (and loving) the return of landlines. “There's something wonderfully nostalgic about a beautiful telephone sitting on a bedside table or tucked into a thoughtfully designed corner of a home,” she says.

Of course, there’s plenty of fun to be had in a “dumb home.” “We have had several adult clients wanting to incorporate their hobby of Legos into their design aesthetic,” reveals Allison Smith of Gem + Clay Interiors in St. Augustine, Florida. “I definitely think this speaks to a desire to return to the analog, and that [old-school] element.”

“We always joke about the Tuscan kitchens from 2005, but they had some good things going on,” says Drew Michael Scott of Lone Fox. If you strip away the “bottles of preserved peppers and gaudy oil-rubbed bronze faucets,” the style was actually rocking a great color palette. Shades like brown, ochre, terracotta, and deep red bring a little bit of the great outdoors inside, plus they can pair nicely with today’s cozy interiors. But let’s keep the overly Italian influences in the past, shall we?

Today’s emerging design lovers are extra—just ask Scott, who has been noticing a considerable uptick in passementerie. “These intricate details were everywhere in the mid-2000s, before the world decided everything should be sleek and gray,” he says. “They’re becoming popular again, and I think they really evoke the attention to detail we saw at the turn of the century.”

Though yesteryear’s tassels, beading, and decorative trim are undeniably fun, using them requires a little bit of strategy. Since there’s a fine line between charming and kitschy, balance these louder, upholstered moments with pared-back wooden pieces.

Goodbye, panel-ready refrigerators and dishwashers: Katie Kiser says stainless steel appliances are so back. According to the Tennessee-based designer, the resurgence is a break from the picture-perfect kitchens older generations have pined over. “Gen-Z values practicality over perfection,” she explains. “I think it will show up in their interiors.” And why stop at appliances when you can bring that lived-in feel to the rest of the space? “They don’t hate clutter the way millennials do,” she adds. “We’ll see details like cookbooks in the kitchens and hall trees with tennis rackets on them.”

Though Scott hasn’t seen the Venetian Gold granite that ruled Y2K just yet, he’s noticing many homeowners are willing to get creative with their countertops.

“We've moved beyond using only Carrara marble in kitchens,” he explains.

Ditto for quartz, which some designers believe is on the outs. Instead, Scott says travertine is huge again and granite is poised to make the comeback. If you want to keep these materials from appearing dated, he encourages you to reach for stones with bolding veining and inclusions.

#6932. Survey Finds 84% of Organizations Reported at Least One AI-Related Incident in the Past Year

Companies are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, with 84% reporting at least one AI-related incident in the past year, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

The company's 2026 Application Access Governance report found that some organizations have experienced incorrect financial transactions and AI actions they couldn't trace or explain. The findings raise concerns about whether enterprises have enough oversight of AI systems that can make decisions and take actions on their own

The findings are based on an independent survey of 1,000 senior finance and finance technology leaders in the United States and United Kingdom. The AI-specific analysis included 879 respondents whose organizations use the technology in critical business applications.

The report found that 35% of surveyed organizations have deployed AI agents capable of taking actions within business applications. However, only 11% of organizations using AI said their governance and security controls were keeping pace with deployment.

Delinea also found gaps in centralized visibility into agent deployment and permissions, including who owns agents and who approved their use.

Some respondents reported segregation-of-duties conflicts involving AI, while others identified agents that continued operating after the employees who deployed them had left the organization.

The research also highlighted gaps in monitoring access across business systems. Only 46% of respondents said they continuously monitor and manage cumulative access risks across applications.

Those gaps can make it difficult to identify segregation-of-duties conflicts, in which a person or agent has permissions across systems that should be kept separate.

Despite those issues, 95% of respondents said they were confident their access controls would withstand scrutiny in an external audit.

However, 26% reported access-related audit findings that had recurred in consecutive years, while 25% said some findings had remained unresolved for three or more years.

Only 22% of organizations had a single named owner for each application, with ownership tracked in one system of record.

Meanwhile, 60% reported an increase over the past two years in the number of critical applications operating outside the scope of access governance or Sarbanes-Oxley Act compliance.

The findings highlight the oversight challenges associated with giving AI agents authority to act within business systems, particularly when organizations cannot consistently identify who owns an agent or trace its actions.

Frank Vukovits, chief security scientist at Delinea, said organizations remain responsible for the actions AI agents take within their systems.

"When an agent can approve a transaction or change permissions, the business owns the outcome. You need to know what every agent can do, who owns it, and who approved it, before an auditor asks or your financials are misstated," Vukovits said.

For the full report, go to the Delinea site.

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

#6931. Kate Middleton and Prince William Begin Their First Overseas Tour in Four Years

The couple returns to India for the Earthshot Prize, which awards £1 million each to five environmental initiatives.

Kate Middleton and Prince William have arrived in India for a three-day official visit, their first joint overseas tour since 2022. Announced by Kensington Palace over the weekend, the November 16–18 trip is in honor of the sixth annual Earthshot Prize awards in Mumbai. It also marks a significant step in the Princess of Wales’s return to public duties following her 2024 cancer diagnosis.

The couple will attend the awards ceremony on Tuesday, November 17, when five environmental initiatives will each receive £1 million to expand their work. Founded by Prince William in 2020, the prize recognizes solutions across five areas, including restoring nature, reducing waste, and addressing climate change. Mumbai is the awards’ first Indian host city, following past editions in London, Boston, Singapore, Cape Town, and Rio de Janeiro.

The royal itinerary extends beyond the ceremony. Kensington Palace said the visit would highlight sustainable urban transportation, responsible manufacturing, youth-led environmental projects, and India’s creative industries. Today, the couple joined cricket great Sachin Tendulkar and local children for a game of street cricket in Ballard Estate, a historic neighborhood in South Mumbai. The Prince and Princess of Wales are also scheduled to attend the inaugural Children’s Earthshot Awards, recognizing environmental solutions developed by young people ages 9 to 17.

The trip is Catherine’s first overseas tour with William since she disclosed her cancer diagnosis in 2024, and her first appearance at an Earthshot ceremony since the Boston awards in 2022. It is also the couple’s second official visit to India, a decade after their April 2016 tour.

#6930. Report: Cyber Criminals Move Toward More Targeted Phishing Attacks

Cyber criminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, increasingly using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4. The security firm found that spear-phishing attacks increased 41% over the past year.

The company's Phishing Threat Trends Report, Vol. 8 found that overall phishing volume increased 29.8% year over year. Organizations received an average of one dangerous e-mail for every 733 messages, but the bigger concern is how attackers are changing their approach.

Rather than sending the same fraudulent message to thousands of people, criminals are spending more time researching their targets and creating convincing messages that appear to come from trusted coworkers, businesses, and technology platforms.

The findings are based on threat data from KnowBe4 Defend, the company's inbound e-mail security platform, which processes approximately 1.15 billion event signals daily.

KnowBe4 found that several traditional phishing methods declined significantly compared with 2025. Mobile-focused scams dropped 47.3%, advance-fee fraud fell 31.3%, and missed-message scams declined 11.3%.

Meanwhile, spear phishing increased 41.1%, pre-attack reconnaissance rose 33.7%, and company impersonation climbed 28%.

The shift suggests that attackers are finding more value in carefully crafted scams that exploit existing business relationships and workplace routines.

Business e-mail compromise (BEC) remains particularly common, accounting for 51.2% of phishing e-mails analyzed in the report. Human resources, finance departments, and executive leadership are among the most frequently targeted groups.

Attackers also appear to be timing their campaigns around normal business activity. KnowBe4 found that 90.4% of phishing messages arrive Monday through Friday, with Monday accounting for the largest share at 20.6%.

One of the report's more troubling findings involves the growing use of legitimate technology platforms to deliver malicious content.

KnowBe4 found that phishing attacks were twice as likely to originate from legitimate infrastructure in the third quarter of 2026 compared with the previous quarter.

That includes AI services, where attackers are taking advantage of trusted domains to host fake developer consoles, login prompts, and credential-stealing pages. The report also identified a 228% increase in AI-brand impersonation attacks between March and August.

Because these malicious pages can operate on legitimate domains with valid security certificates, users may not see the usual warning signs associated with suspicious websites.

"For years, security advice has focused on a predictable checklist: Check the sender address, hover over the link, and look for the padlock," said Jack Chapman, senior vice president of threat intelligence at KnowBe4.

"While still foundational, that guidance is becoming less useful by the day."

The findings echo a recent report about management software and AI e-mail assistants. Research from Fortra and Barracuda showed how criminals are using legitimate administrative tools and hidden AI instructions to manipulate users and compromise systems.

Anthropic announced it is introducing machine-readable watermarks to text generated by supported Claude models as it moves to comply with European Union AI transparency rules.

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

Sixteen companies were selected as winners for their product achievements.

Microsoft has published a draft code of conduct that lays out how its homegrown AI models should behave, with human control taking priority over model capability, autonomy, and even task completion.

#6927. At the Frick Collection, Louis Vuitton Finds Itself Among the Masters

The French house’s sponsorship of the Frick includes a fashion show and a landmark exhibition of Italian bronzes.

Above: Louis Vuitton kicked off a three-year partnership with New York’s Frick Museum with its cruise show in May.

Bright lights go up, Peaches’ “Boys Wanna Be Her” starts pounding, and a girl wearing layers of denim begins strutting past such luminaries as Zendaya, Chloë Sevigny, and Ava DuVernay. In many ways a normal New York fashion event—except the rooms are adorned with Renaissance paintings, Qing Dynasty vases, and 19th-century gilded French furniture.

The exalted surroundings are in fact the galleries of the Frick Collection, the Gilded Age treasure trove that was recently reenvisioned with the help of Elle Decor A-List architect Annabelle Selldorf. The fashion, music, and glamour are courtesy of Louis Vuitton, which has made a tradition of working with cultural institutions and heritage sites. The French house kicked off a three-year sponsorship of the Frick with this show in May, and now the first exhibition with the company’s support is open: a landmark show of Italian bronze sculptural masterpieces.

The reliefs, figurines, medals, and candelabra were drawn from collections as wide-ranging as Dresden, London, Washington, and Siena, the focus of the show. Donatello’s tomb of the Bishop of Grosseto, a life-size traffic stopper of a slab, timeworn yet still lyrical, is worth a visit alone, as is the miniature, gilded zinc brass relief of Bellerophon and the Chimera by Francesco di Giorgio. In an era of endless swiping, the lesson of the Chimera—and of these timeless bronzes—endures: be wary of desire’s fast fulfillment. Instead, wander into the rooms and stay a while.

Now that Amazon’s 12-day Black Friday sale is here, I’m creating an official guide to the iPad deals you should shop now...and which are worth waiting a little longer for.

It comes in three storage sizes—128, 256, and 512GB—with the best discount on the 128GB model.

All in all, the iPad deals available right now aren’t worth writing home about—but don’t despair! These deals can literally change in an instant, and I’m predicting that prices will become comparable to last year’s Black Friday sale if you’re willing to wait until next week. #6924. Study: Sign-In Problems Are Primary Drivers of Help Desk Tickets

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a new study from ControlUp.

Analyzing 1,069,110 help desk tickets, the IT operations company identified password resets, account lockouts, and multifactor authentication (MFA) issues as major drivers of support demand.

At one large enterprise, account-related tickets took a median of 11 minutes to resolve, compared with 50 hours for network problems. Meanwhile, 87% of network and phone tickets were reassigned between teams, compared with 20% of account tickets.

MFA enrollment and re-enrollment problems appeared at 40% of organizations, with one in five linking these issues to employees replacing or upgrading their phones. Virtual desktop problems appeared at 55% of organizations analyzed.

Traditional phone support remained a major channel for IT assistance.

Among organizations that recorded how support requests were submitted, 57% reported that phone calls accounted for approximately two-thirds or more of contacts. At one organization, that figure reached 99%.

Machine-generated tickets from monitoring, security, backup, and patching systems appeared at half of the organizations studied. At 15%, automated tickets represented between 25% and 50% of all requests.

The research covered enterprises across retail, healthcare, financial services, manufacturing, construction, energy, and IT services.

ControlUp said the findings highlight the need to evaluate recurring problems and their operational impact rather than relying solely on ticket volume and resolution speed.

"IT organizations have traditionally measured support performance by how quickly they close tickets, but that doesn't tell the whole story," said Simon Townsend, senior vice president of marketing and office of the CTO at ControlUp.

"A password reset that takes a few minutes and an infrastructure failure that disrupts an entire location shouldn't carry the same operational weight."

For more information, go to the ControlUp site.

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

#6923. A Kitchen in Bloom: How Tiffany Howell and Miele Reimagine the Heart of the Home

Eschewing conventional design for an moody, romantic kitchen, this wildly glamorous space proves that high-tech convenience can foster intimacy, slowness, and the art of living well.

When Tiffany Howell of Night Palm Interiors designs a home, her vision transcends conventional norms, veering into an ethereal realm—an approach she calls “the poetry of place.” In Howell’s mind, film, fashion, and music are as essential as decorative art, and she uses these inspirations to create theatrical spaces that ignite all the senses—layering texture, sound, scent, light, and form to evoke deep emotion. When executed with this level of intention, a room becomes an environment you don't merely look at but fundamentally feel.

When Miele partnered with Howell to create a dream kitchen for an installation at Art Basel, she seized the opportunity to reimagine what a kitchen can be. The result is the interactive 360-degree rendering seen here—a wildly imaginative digital environment that inspired the brand’s upcoming physical installation at Miami’s premier art fair. “I wanted an undeniable sexiness, a beautiful, sensory experience,” she says. “I created the space with characters in mind that I’d invite to a dinner party in this kitchen.” After all, the kitchen is a home’s heartbeat, an interactive space with multiple purposes.

“I do think the kitchen is really one of the most tender rooms in the house,” Howell says. She leaned into that romance by installing black cabinetry with blossoming branches and birds in iridescent greens and pinks. The cabinets are an homage to her love of 1920s chinoiserie furniture and fashion, seen through a very glamorous, Los Angeles lens. That California focus also meant bringing the outdoors in. The grand conservatory space harnesses diffused, natural light with the promise of the scent of jasmine wafting into the room. Howell’s ideal playlist sets the tone with jazz greats and ambient synth-pop.

“I wanted to create a kitchen that’s a completely sensory experience,” Howell explains. “The sound of a crackling warm fireplace, the aroma of a meal cooked for loved ones, or of making coffee in the morning.” For Howell, these aren't incidental details; they are the sensory architecture that turns raw space into lived emotion. In Howell’s view, this kitchen is “also a place where you can take a nap, read a book, or write in your journal. Every moment in this room can become a ritualistic experience of living well.”

When it came to the practical needs of a kitchen, Howell used Miele as a muse, an invitation to eschew the pale minimalism so common in luxury kitchens. “The appliances are so elegant, they kind of act as the jewelry of the room,” she says. “And their functionality and ease of operation give you back time to live better, relax with a drink, hang out with family and friends, and create the stories that make up a life.”

Consider the Miele Built-In Coffee System and wine cooler in matching dark finishes which are nearly camouflaged so the technology reads more as furniture than appliance. That tension between heirloom design and high-tech styling becomes a unified part of a lived-in, well-collected home. Accent pieces, including a vintage Fortuny pendant lamp over the kitchen island, and upholstered chairs in the conservatory with detailing reminiscent of antique lingerie, create a moody environment well suited to Miele's black-finished lines that resemble rich obsidian.

The 36” Full Surface Induction Cooktop features a frameless, flush design that disappears into a dark-veined countertop. Transforming the entire glass surface into one expansive cooking area, it fits up to six pots or pans at once and with Intelligent Pan Recognition and SmartSelect White controls seamlessly transfer power settings wherever pans are moved. On the same wall is the 30” Handleless Combi-Steam Oven XXL, with DualSteam technology that emanates ASMR sounds of steam cooking, and a black glass façade that resembles a framed panel or window. From the cool, flush glide of the induction cooktop to the whisper-quiet hum of precision steam, the technology engages the senses rather than interrupting them, turning modern convenience into an intuitive, tactile ritual. Rather than breaking up the wall with a stack of conspicuous appliances, these seamless integrations keep the architecture artful. And for added convenience, Miele’s elegant touch controls are enhanced with Wi-Fi connectivity for an easier user experience.

Howell’s concealment of technology is exhibited in her use of the 42” French Door Refrigerator with its elongated custom handles and a dark lacquered paneled front that echoes the floral chinoiserie motifs—an unconventional solution that turns a mundane appliance into a piece of decorative art. Once the refrigerator doors are open, the Brilliant Light Pro illumination makes it easy to survey the food inside the large capacity interior.

“How do you nurture yourself and others in the kitchen?” Howell asks. Her answer: a design that creates a restorative space evoking intimacy and slowness, where technology can encourage a lightness and give us back more time. By composing every sight, surface, scent, and sound into a single harmonious narrative, Howell proves that a kitchen shouldn't just be an aesthetic statement—it should be something you feel in your bones. “What excites me is building a new world with more possibilities,” Howell says. “I don’t think I could design any other way.”

#6922. 13 Clever Details I Saw at the 2026 Kips Bay Decorator Show House in Dallas

Check out the first ever home gym in Kips Bay Show House history.

The seventh annual Kips Bay Decorator Show House in Dallas returns to 2999 Turtle Creek Boulevard, but don’t expect it to look anything like it did in 2025. A new group of the country’s leading designers has taken on the 25,000-square-foot French Renaissance estate, transforming each room to showcase bold ideas, highlight exceptional craftsmanship, and reveal the upcoming trends we can expect to see in the world of interior design.

Visit the show house and see each of the 22 designers’ rooms, from the first dedicated home gym in Kips Bay history by Dallas-based Platinum Gym Design, to a small but mighty European-inspired kitchen by Stephenson Home, to a 1970s-inspired primary bathroom fit with a glass brick shower and curtained-off water closet by Nuance Interiors. With sponsors like Benjamin Moore, The Shade Store, The Rug Company, and more, the vibrant and personality-filled spaces come as no surprise.

As always, proceeds from the show house go to the Kips Bay Boys & Girls Club, which provides educational, enrichment, and developmental programs to thousands of young people, as well as Dallas-based nonprofits Dwell with Dignity and The Crystal Charity Ball.

To see this celebration of exceptional design, you can purchase tickets here and visit the house from Saturday, November 14th to Sunday, December 6th. However, if you can’t make it to the Lone Star State in time, check out some of our favorite moments from the event below.

Kelsi Lamberth of Nuance Interiors pulled inspiration from the 1970s for her primary bathroom, separating the shower from the main prep space using a curved glass brick wall. The sculptural aspects can be found throughout in this groovy space.

In the primary bedroom, Troy Rivington of Rivington Marx Interiors cocooned the room in a dark floral wallpaper and accessorized with other moody botanicals throughout. He was inspired by the romance of 1940s Cairo, dressing the room up like it belonged to a well-traveled collector.

As the first ever dedicated home gym at the Kips Bay Show House, Peter Hoffman of Platinum Gym Design chose a refined, luxurious setup. He named the room Tanren after the Japanese art of hammering and refining metal, and used walnut in both the equipment and the ceiling slats, stainless steel frames, and brown leather accents to complete the exercise space.

In this bespoke bathroom, Russ Peters and Bill Cates of Peters Cates Design lined the wall behind the Studio McGee x Kohler bathtub with a rich merlot tile by RIAD Tile. It’s a beautiful statement and a fabulous way to bring more texture and dimension to the room.

In this tucked away library space, filled with collected finds, designers Christina Garcia Lysaught and Grayson Knight of Layered Dimensions finished off this dark space with a stunning statement mirror. Around the perimeter are resin busts of wildlife, like owls and deer, bringing in a bejeweled touch to the room.

The small but mighty kitchen, designed by Katie Stephenson of Stephenson Home, is crowned with a crackled-gold blooming wallpaper, bringing a warm balance to the rest of the room. It’s covered in birds, butterflies, and blossoms, and it wraps the cook space in a golden glow, reminding all who enter that this room should be as beautiful as it is functional.

The citron paint color wrapping the walls in Jen Bienvenu’s entrance hall is undoubtedly enchanting and serves as the perfect backdrop for her “After the Hunt” theme, but it’s the lampshades made from old silk saris that tie everything together for us. They provide such a magical warmth and really capture the essence of a collector’s treasure room.

The rosy palette in designer Jen Dean’s—founder of Jede Interiors—grand bathroom is topped off with a hand-painted floral mural that covers the ceiling. The rose-gold shine reflects the natural light in the room and give it more depth, while the natural motifs spread across the rest of the space.

Inside this observatory bedroom by sisters Amy Mobley and Katie Norrid of City Hill Design, the Verellen bed is raised up from the ground on a custom platform, emphasizing the importance of the furniture’s role in this space meant for relaxation and rest. It’s wrapped in a striped silk carpet that beckons your touch with a chartreuse mohair sofa at the foot.

Related Story: A Trip to the Lone Star State Inspired the Earthy Textures of This Tennessee New Build

Colorful and collected, this vibrant library by designer Betsy Wentz is not visually quiet. The maximalist space is covered in pattern-mixed Schumacher from ceiling to floor, and the designer pumped up the volume by opting for this triangular table in this tucked away corner.

In designer Amy Switzer’s textural bar lounge—filled with plush chairs, unique artwork, large tassel accessories, and beaded cigarettes—it’s the stone details that really make the room sing. Onyx cornices crown the windows, blue agate inlays decorate the floor, and a banquette which curves around a custom stone and wood table bring a natural coolness to the room that only stone can bring.

#6920. What Watch Will Robert Pattinson Wear as James Bond? It's Complicated.

Timepieces are almost as much a part of 007’s wardrobe as his dinner jackets.

Perhaps you've heard? There's a new James Bond in town!

Robert Pattinson has been announced as the next 007, and while most of the speculation has understandably focused on how the former vampire/current Batman might approach Britain's foremost secret agent, watch aficionados are asking another question: What will he wear on his wrist?

Watches, after all, are almost as much a part of Bond's wardrobe as his dinner jackets, Aston Martins, and vodka martinis. Sean Connery famously wore a Rolex Submariner, Roger Moore experimented with Seiko's digital watches, and Omega has been 007's watch of choice since Pierce Brosnan strapped on a trusty Seamaster in GoldenEye in 1995. That brand remains, of course, a paid partner of the franchise.

Yesterday's reveal showed no watch on the newly anointed 007's wrist, and Pattinson's own taste in watches is rather different from the rugged, sporty timepieces traditionally associated with Bond.

In fact, he doesn't seem particularly keen on big watches at all.

"Whenever a watch gets too thick and it keeps your cuff up, I don't really understand the appeal," he told one interviewer as recently September. "A lot of guys really like that, but I don't think I can really pull that off. I need to only have a half reveal."

And when asked what he looks for in a watch, he was even more specific.

"I definitely like really thin watches. That's the main thing. In general, I would probably prefer a leather strap."

Which is quite a contrast with the substantial divers we've become accustomed to seeing on Bond's wrist.

The fact is, until recently, Pattinson wasn't much of a watch enthusiast at all. That changed at this year's Oscars, when he wore a then-unreleased rose-gold Jaeger-LeCoultre Master Control Chronomètre Perpetual Calendar. Other actors apparently began asking him about it at the afterparty, and something clicked.

He subsequently became an ambassador for Jaeger-LeCoultre, although his relationship with the brand goes back much further. As a teenager, he borrowed his mother's Reverso to impress his schoolmates. More recently, he's revealed that Bruce Wayne will wear a Reverso in the next Batman film.

On screen, Pattinson's watch choices have been interesting. He wore a Chanel J12 in David Cronenberg's Cosmopolis, while Christopher Nolan's Tenet, in which he played a mysterious secret agent, featured a Bond-adjacent Omega De Ville.

Meanwhile, the announcement has prompted some entertaining activity on Instagram. Within hours of Pattinson being named Bond, Jaeger-LeCoultre posted pictures of its new ambassador wearing the Master Control Chronomètre Perpetual Calendar. Omega, meanwhile, posted an image of its James Bond Seamaster. And today, Jaeger-LeCoultre has been back with more Pattinson pictures.

It's a little social-media tit-for-tat, although there's no reason the two relationships can't coexist. For now, at least, there's a straightforward distinction: Pattinson can wear whatever he likes. Bond has his own watch.

#6919. Enterprise AI Spending Outpaces Adoption Growth, Report Finds

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

The venture capital firm's 2026 State of Enterprise AI report estimates deployment spending reached a $176.8 billion annualized run rate by September, up from $33 billion in 2025. The share of enterprises paying for AI rose from 55% to 65%.

A larger bill can reflect broader use inside an organization, but it does not independently establish that the additional work is useful or profitable.

Menlo's methodology describes revenue figures as annualized run rates. The $176.8 billion estimate therefore should not be read as $176.8 billion already spent during the first nine months of 2026.

The report also distinguishes unusually heavy spenders from the typical respondent. Average reported spending reached 4.7 times the prior year's level, while the median reached 2.2 times. That gap suggests a smaller group of large spenders drove the average substantially.

The practical implication is that an industry growth figure may be a poor benchmark for an individual organization's budget. Buyers need to understand their own workload growth and the results it produces.

"Agents are changing the economics of software," Menlo principal Derek Xiao said in the firm's announcement.

Menlo reports that agents account for 80% of tokens on OpenRouter, a service providing access to multiple models. That measures activity on one platform, rather than all enterprise AI use. Token consumption also measures processing volume, rather than completed business outcomes.

An agent that makes repeated attempts can consume more resources without delivering proportionately more value. For procurement teams, the relevant comparison is consequently the cost of completing acceptable work, including the review it requires.

Menlo estimates that Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google capture 69% of enterprise AI deployment spending. That measure is distinct from their shares of enterprise language-model API spending, a narrower category. The figures should not be used interchangeably.

The report draws on a Morning Consult survey of nearly 400 U.S. enterprise decision-makers responsible for more than $4 billion in AI spending. Market totals are modeled estimates informed by surveys, public filings, and third-party reports. Menlo discloses investments in Anthropic and OpenRouter.

A separate announcement from Information Services Group describes buyers putting more emphasis on the economics and reliability of production deployments. ISG says ongoing security and observability work remains necessary as organizations scale.

That provides a useful distinction between market demand and business value. Suppliers can generate growing revenue while customers still determine which deployments justify the cost.

For enterprise leaders, the new spending estimate strengthens the case for examining usage beyond license counts. It leaves the harder purchasing question with the customer: How much worthwhile work did the increased spending actually buy?

Menlo's State of Enterprise AI report is available here.

John K. Waters is the editor in chief of a number of Converge360.com sites, with a focus on high-end development, AI and future tech. He's been writing about cutting-edge technologies and culture of Silicon Valley for more than two decades, and he's written more than a dozen books. He also co-scripted the documentary film Silicon Valley: A 100 Year Renaissance, which aired on PBS.  He can be reached at [email protected].

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

#6918. ‘Off Campus’ Season 2 Might Make a Major Departure From the Books, Thanks to This Casting Shakeup

The absence could change a key storyline from the books in the Prime Video series.

Fans are already counting down the days until we see Allie and Dean’s love story pan out on Off Campus season 2, and I don’t blame them one bit. It’ll be a minute until we reunite with the Briar U gang, but fans are keeping up with every update on the TV adaptation of Elle Kennedy’s book series in the meantime. And the latest news on season 2 is that a certain cast member won’t be returning.

“I’m not gonna be in the next season,” Riley Davis, who plays Allie’s ex-boyfriend Sean on the Prime Video series, told People at the New York City premiere of his movie November 1963 on November 18.

As for the reason behind his exit? Riley says there were some scheduling conflicts—which checks out, since he has a role in another YA series, Netflix’s Crew Girl. It’s unclear whether that particular project played a part in his departure from Off Campus.

“There was a plan for some Sean in season 2, and we just had a bit of a scheduling thing on my side and a bit on their side,” he explained. “So we’ll see what happens down the line.”

What’s more: Riley’s exit could potentially signal that the second season of Off Campus might go a different direction from the book series it’s based on.

As viewers saw in the show’s first season, Riley’s character, Sean, plays a pivotal role in Allie and Dean’s romance. In the books, he and Allie have an on-again, off-again relationship—but we might not see that pan out now that Riley is out.

This isn’t the first time the TV adaptation shook things up with some changes from the source material. In season 1, the show’s writers introduced John Logan’s nonbinary sibling Jules Logan, who noticeably wasn’t in the OG book series.

We’ll have to see how Riley/Sean’s absence is really handled when Off Campus season 2 hits Prime Video on April 28, 2027!

#6914. Phishing Threats Totaled 7.3 Billion in Q3 2026

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

According to a report from Microsoft Threat Intelligence and the Microsoft Defender Security Research Team, phishing detections reached nearly 3 billion in September alone.

The findings also highlighted changes in attacker techniques, including business-service impersonation, credential theft, and social engineering through Microsoft Teams.

The Redmond-based firm recognized approximately 129 million malicious messages spoofing the sender domains of tracked business services during Q3.

DocuSign was the most frequently spoofed brand in September, accounting for 29% of the month's tracked spoofing activity. Adobe, SurveyMonkey, and Barracuda also experienced increases toward the end of the quarter.

The research found that some malicious messages were delivered despite failing e-mail authentication checks and being identified as threats.

In September, tenant- or user-level security overrides applied to 63% of these messages with a recorded security outcome — most involved tenant-level exceptions.

Microsoft warned that overly broad exceptions intended to support legitimate business communications can weaken protection against impersonation attacks.

The company emphasized that these findings do not establish whether recipients opened the messages or whether accounts were compromised.

Microsoft also recorded approximately 141,000 malicious Teams call attempts during Q3.

Attackers increasingly used ordinary personal names instead of explicitly impersonating IT support personnel. Such display names accounted for 46% of an observed sample in July, rising to 88% in September.

Meanwhile, QR code phishing declined 67% compared to Q2, totaling roughly 12.1 million attacks.

CAPTCHA-gated phishing activity increased toward the end of the quarter, reaching 3.1 million detected attacks in September. SVG attachments played a significant role in the increase.

Credential theft remained the primary objective of malicious attachment campaigns, with approximately 94% to 96% of payload-based attacks directing users to phishing sites or fraudulent sign-in pages.

Microsoft recommended reviewing Exchange Online Protection and Defender for Office 365 configurations, enabling Safe Links and Safe Attachments, and strengthening authentication protections.

The company also advised organizations to review security exceptions and verify the identities of external Teams contacts before responding to suspicious requests.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

Anthropic announced it is introducing machine-readable watermarks to text generated by supported Claude models as it moves to comply with European Union AI transparency rules.

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

Sixteen companies were selected as winners for their product achievements.

Microsoft has published a draft code of conduct that lays out how its homegrown AI models should behave, with human control taking priority over model capability, autonomy, and even task completion.

#6911. Who Is Daddy Franklin From ‘The Body’? For Starters, Nnamdi Asomugha Is Kerry Washington’s Husband

Daddy Franklin is married to Olivia Pope?

If you watched The Body in one sitting and are now fully obsessed with it, you’re not alone!! The horrifying coming-of-age series is scary good, and while you may not be familiar with the actor who plays Father Samuel Franklin (aka Daddy Franklin), you definitely know his very famous wife. As it turns out, Nnamdi Asomugha has been married to Kerry Washington for over 13 years now, having fallen in love after watching Kerry perform in Race on Broadway in 2010. Kerry later confirmed the story during a 2018 interview with Marie Claire, saying, “The last time I did theater, it completely transformed my life. That’s where I met my husband.” Cute!!!

Though things got off to a rocky start (Kerry apparently told Nnamdi she didn’t date athletes, and Nnamdi told Kerry he didn’t date actors), the two got together anyway, and just about three years later in June 2013, they tied the knot in a super low-key ceremony. According to their marriage license, Kerry and Nnamdi said “I do” in Hailey, Idaho, where Nnamdi’s sister officiated and Kerry’s parents served as witnesses. Since then, the couple has welcomed two kids together—Isabelle Amarachi, born in 2014, and Caleb Kelechi, born in 2016—and are seemingly thriving outside of the spotlight. But we think it’s time to learn a lil bit more about Nnamdi, don’t you?

Here’s everything you should know about the man behind Daddy Franklin, including how he rose to fame as a former NFL cornerback and what he’s up to next.

After growing up in Louisiana in a Nigerian Igbo household, Nnamdi attended the University of California, Berkeley, where he played for the California Golden Bears football team. Soon after graduating, Nnamdi was drafted in the first round of the 2003 NFL Draft by the Oakland Raiders, which is very impressive.

Over his 11-year professional football career, Nnamdi also played for the Philadelphia Eagles and the San Francisco 49ers, and in December 2013, the cornerback signed a one-day contract with Oakland so he could retire as a Raider. I love to see a loyal man!

According to Nnamdi, the decision to retire at 32 was difficult, but it still felt like the right time for him. “Yeah, I felt that it was a good time, but it didn’t make it any easier,” he told UPROXX. “It’s a game that 'til this day I miss, and I miss playing. It’s not that I want to go play again, but I miss so many aspects of it; being around the team, being around the coaching, that calm before the storm.”

Nnamdi first caught the acting bug all the way back in 2008, when he landed the role of “Party Guest with Wife” on the CW sitcom The Game. (Remember: There are no small roles, only small actors!!) He then caught the eye of director Peter Berg on the set of a Nike commercial shoot in 2009, who was impressed by the athlete’s ease in front of the camera. From there, Nnamdi went on to play small roles in Friday Night Lights, Leverage, and Cubed before making his on-screen feature film debut in 2012’s Fire with Fire.

Since retiring from the NFL, Nnamdi has fully committed himself to his acting career with starring roles in the films Crown Heights, Sylvie’s Love, The Good Nurse, The Knife, and—most recently—The Body. He’s also served as a producer on several of those projects as well.

Despite all his success, Nnamdi still remains as modest as ever, avoiding social media and press to instead focus on his craft. “I’m really bad at self-promotion. I know that about myself,” he previously explained to Bleacher Report back in 2017. “People told me all of the time, ‘You could be such a big star if you just talked about yourself more,’ but I’m not good at that. It’s always been about team.”

During a 2018 interview with Today, Kerry corrected a host when they alluded to her two children, saying, “I’m a mother of three." The reason: Nnamdi has a now-teenage daughter from another relationship, who Kerry has helped raise alongside her two biological children. And just like with Isabelle and Caleb, the couple keeps their eldest far from the paparazzi, so very little is known about her.

As Kerry joked to People, “I have moments where I take pictures or videos of one of my three amazing kids and I wanna post it online, and I tend to just send it to my parents or my shrink instead.”

Philanthropy is important to Nnamdi, which is why he created the Asomugha Foundation in 2005 at the very beginning of his career.

The foundation has two main programs: Orphans and Widows in Need (which provides food, shelter, medicine, vocational training, literacy efforts, and scholarships to widows and orphans in Nigeria) and Asomugha College Tour for Scholars (which takes selected students from Bay Area and Los Angeles area high schools on college tours across the country).

Though Kerry and Nnamdi are both actors and producers, it isn’t often that they work together on creative projects. Back in 2017, Nnamdi told Vanity Fair that it was possible he and his wife would co-produce a project at some point.

“We would produce,” he said. “We’d probably produce before acting, I would think.” The following year, his production company, iAm21 Entertainment, helped produce American Son, a Broadway play starring Kerry, and hopefully, that won’t be the last time the two are involved in the same project.

As far as sharing the screen goes, however, Nnamdi doesn’t think that will ever happen. As he told The New York Times, “I produced American Son, but as actors, there’s no plan as of now for that collaboration. We’re very supportive of each other’s journeys, but we’ve always been that way. We always want the best for each other in whatever we’re doing.”

Case in point? The couple’s *chef’s kiss* appearance at the 2025 Met Gala. Ahem:

Oh, and the duo’s 2025 Golden Globes looks:

And ofc, he was right by Kerry's side during her December 2024 Hollywood Walk of Fame ceremony, where she finally received her well-deserved star. How cute do they look with Kerry's parents??

TL;DR: Kerry and Nnamdi truly couldn’t be any cuter. Keep on being adorably low-key, you two.

#6907. Designers Say "Cook's Tables" Are About to Replace Kitchen Islands in 2027

Their versatility is just part of their appeal.

Pictured Above: A kitchen designed by Amy Sklar.

For years, oversized kitchen islands have been the be-all and end-all, but lately, designers and homeowners are gravitating toward something a bit smaller: a cook’s table.

Unlike built-in islands that can sometimes dominate a kitchen and cover most of the available floor space, cook’s tables bring a lot of flexibility to the room. Because they’re freestanding, you can move them around depending on your needs (especially if they’re on wheels), whether that’s for prepping meals, hanging out with your morning coffee, or helping kids with homework after school. They also tend to be narrower, making them the perfect solution for small kitchens.

The other benefit is the big charm factor they add. They’re often wooden, with traditional-looking design elements, like turned legs and curvy silhouettes. Cooks tables can also frequently be found in vintage and antique stores, so they’re a great way to work a bit of charm and patina into an otherwise utilitarian space.

As kitchens move away from ultra-sleek and minimal, pieces with warmth and character are becoming more and more popular, and a cook’s table is the perfect example of that. If you’re considering adding one to your own home, learn what to look for—and how to style it—below.

One of the biggest reasons cook’s tables are trending right now is that they soften the overall look and feel of a kitchen. “A traditional island can dominate a kitchen,” says designer Amy Sklar. Because cook’s tables don’t have a bulky built-in base, they feel visually lighter and less imposing, especially in kitchens where space is limited.

Designers also point to the emotional shift happening in homes post-pandemic. “The pandemic reshaped the way we live at home, placing greater value on spaces designed for connection and togetherness,” says designer Becky Ducsik of The Phinery. “Nowhere is this more apparent than in the kitchen, where homeowners are reimagining how they gather and entertain.”

Unlike traditional islands that are packed with appliances and storage, cook’s tables create a more communal focal point centered around gathering and spending time together. That relaxed atmosphere is part of what makes them feel so appealing. Designer Cynthia Lananh notes that cook’s tables strike a balance between practicality and personality, functioning as prep space, dining space, and entertaining hub all at once. “I love a kitchen island that looks like a piece of furniture,” she says, adding that the look feels more casual, approachable, and timeless than a standard built-in island.

While cook’s tables can absolutely make a statement in a large kitchen, designers say they’re especially effective in kitchens with tighter footprints or galley-style layouts. Because they take up less visual and physical space than a traditional island, they can help smaller kitchens feel more open and airy.

That said, larger kitchens can benefit from them too, just in a different way. “With the right vintage find or custom-crafted piece, it can anchor the room and become a striking centerpiece that adds both function and timeless appeal,” says Ducsik.

She recommends using cook’s tables as the focal point in kitchens where storage and appliances can be concentrated along the perimeter. Since seating typically wraps around all sides, she suggests leaving at least four to five feet of clearance around the table to maintain a comfortable flow.

For homeowners who want the look but don’t have space for a freestanding table in the center of the room, Ducsik also suggests integrating one as a peninsula extension at the end of cabinetry for a similar communal feel.

Unlike built-in islands, cook’s tables offer a level of flexibility that’s hard to beat. Since they’re freestanding, they can be moved or adapted more easily over time—a feature designers say is especially useful for entertaining.

Sklar points out that cook’s tables can create a flexible landing zone within a kitchen and are ideal for holidays or large gatherings when extra room is needed. “This is perfect for holidays and large gatherings where you need even more space,” Lananh says. She recommends adding casters if the table will double as both a prep and dining space, making it easier to move around as needed.

Scale is one of the most important things to get right: The table should feel proportional to the room while still leaving enough space to comfortably move around it. Height matters, too. Sklar notes that many vintage tables sit lower than standard counter height, so homeowners may need to modify them slightly with casters or leg extenders to make them more functional for prep work.

As for materials, wood remains the go-to choice for warmth and character. “Durable woods (like oak, maple, or walnut) are popular for their warmth, strength, and character,” says Lananh. “Reclaimed wood adds rustic charm, whereas metal or butcher block tops offer a more durable, industrial aesthetic.”

Ducsik echoes those sentiments and suggests echoing the table’s wood tones throughout the kitchen with details like open shelving, cutting boards, or serving pieces to create a cohesive look.

Lastly, focus on shape. Softer corners and curves can make the space feel more intimate, while rectangular tables can maximize seating and prep space. Ultimately, the goal is for the piece to feel like furniture, not cabinetry. And that’s exactly what makes cook’s tables so appealing in the first place.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.
