#6859. Lafayette 148’s Emily Smith on 30 Years in Fashion and Finding Her Uniform

In ELLE’s Office Hours series, we ask people in powerful positions about their first jobs, worst jobs, and everything in between. This month, we sat down with Lafayette 148 creative director Emily Smith as the New York-based fashion house celebrates its 30th anniversary. Smith has spent more than two decades at the helm of the brand, working her way up from a part-time freelancer to creative lead. But her fashion ambitions began long before she arrived in New York. Growing up in Atlanta, she ran her own neighborhood art camp before discovering designers like Donna Karan and realizing fashion could be a career. Now, Smith is celebrating the women who have shaped Lafayette 148 through its “Uniform” campaign, which highlights accomplished New Yorkers and their individual approaches to personal style. Below, Smith reflects on her unconventional start, why she’s stayed at the company for over 20 years, and the career advice she wishes she’d followed sooner.

I ran a camp in my neighborhood because, at the time, I thought I wanted to be an art teacher. It was basically babysitting all the neighborhood kids, but I created a flyer, got paid for it, and did the whole thing. I started in second grade, and we did it every year for about eight years. I even started hiring people so we could have two classes going at once. We had swimming lessons. The local news even came and did a segment on it. I loved decorating my classroom and setting everything up. My parents were so nice to give me the garage and basement to do it all. Not necessarily an official job, but my first money-making moment.

I was a bagger at Kroger. It just was not for me.

I was always creative growing up. At a certain point, I discovered Donna Karan. It’s kind of crazy when you see somebody doing something and suddenly realize, Oh, that’s a career. I can do that. I became obsessed with everything in the ’90s, all the New York fashion at the time. That was when I decided I wanted to pursue it. When college applications came around, my mom really encouraged me to go to art school. I started in fashion, and that became my focus. Growing up in Atlanta, I wasn’t necessarily surrounded by a big fashion community. But my dad’s office was across the street from the art museum at one point, and I remember going there and having that exposure. My mom was always crafty and creative, too. She really encouraged us to be who we were.

There’s a language creative people have that’s a little different from the way business people communicate. When I went to art school, I felt like I’d found my people. It wasn’t just fashion people. It was photographers, fine artists, and all these different kinds of creatives. Being around people who thought about things differently was really inspiring. Even today, I thrive around people who have creative insight. There’s always a little more depth to the way they see things.

I interned at Bill Blass for about six months. I moved to New York in July 2001, and we had our fashion show on September 10. I remember Marc Jacobs had his show that day, too, and everybody was celebrating. People were saying, “Just come a little late to work tomorrow.” And then, obviously, September 11 happened. A week later, the entire team was let go, from the top down. What I learned from that experience was the fragility of everything. Don’t take anything for granted. Anything can come and go in a moment. But I also worked with Lars Nilsson and Hervé Pierre Braillard, who were just the kindest people. This industry has a little bit of everything, but I loved that they led with kindness, even when they were under so much pressure. As an intern, I was literally carrying ironing boards down the street and doing a sketch here or there. It was very much about doing whatever needed to be done.

After my internship, I had a brief stint of freelancing. Those were some long months. I remember coming home from one job and my roommates asking how my day was. I said, “Well, I was putting pigs on socks.” I knew I wouldn’t be going back. Freelancing is hard. It’s a grind, but you meet a lot of great people. I actually made some really good contacts during that time. I eventually met Deirdre [Quinn], Lafayette 148’s founder. She met with me on a Saturday, which I thought was incredible. I was really just looking for advice. I wasn’t looking for a job, and I didn’t even know much about the brand. It was only five years old. She was so kind, full of knowledge, and passionate. After I spent a year freelancing and meeting people who seemed so over the industry, her enthusiasm reminded me why I wanted to do this in the first place. She offered me a part-time freelance position, and the rest is history.

In the beginning, security was really important to me. The idea of losing a job and having to pay rent was scary, so that was definitely part of it initially. But really, it was the growth. Within a couple of years, they were sending me to the factory. It was a small, tight-knit company, so I was able to wear a lot of different hats. I had a mentor named Barbara Gast who was traveling back and forth from Italy, and I was learning so much from her. I’d compare notes with friends in the industry and realize how much hands-on experience I was getting. I understood the value of that and really loved being so close to the product. It never got boring. And whenever things did get slightly boring, I’d ask, “Okay, what’s next?” And there was always something. It’s such an entrepreneurial place that you can almost create your next opportunity. That’s what made it so exciting.

I think if you asked Deirdre, she’d have a different answer because she has her own experience of building the company. For me, it’s about having started at a company that was really a factory and being part of the journey of building its identity and what it stands for. I was the kid running up and down the factory floors collecting samples. That hustle and bustle was so innate to who the brand was. To me, it was really exciting to build more of a brand story around that. Thirty years later, we’re still celebrating those roots and making sure we haven’t strayed too far from them, while continuing to evolve. Being part of that journey has been really exciting.

The constant has always been this idea of an accomplished woman. She’s always been at the forefront of who we’re designing for. At the time, it was the career woman. But now, what does that even mean anymore? What does that look like? There are so many different versions of that woman and so many different ways people dress. At the heart of it, she’s a woman who knows what she wants. She’s busy and has a really full life, whether she’s starting her career or ending it. They can all find something in the clothes.

For us, it was about choosing people who inspire us. Number one, they had to be New York women. That was the base. We wanted women who make us want to be better ourselves. From someone like Deborah Berke, the architect, to women who have built incredible careers in other fields, it’s important to shine a light on them. I think back to when I was a kid and how much you need that kind of inspiration. Even at a certain stage in your career, when you’re wondering what the point is, it’s nice to have people who inspire you. These are women who have really built something for themselves, and I think that’s worth celebrating.

Sophie de Rakoff said something during the project that I really liked, and I’m going to steal it: “Dressing with intention.” When you know yourself, you know what works on you. It’s no longer about dressing to impress. There’s a confidence in knowing what works for you and making it work. We’re all so busy. Who has time to overthink getting dressed? You know what works, and you build a system around that. You’re able to create a wardrobe that really works for you. Everybody has a different definition of a uniform, and that was the whole point of the project.

When I first started in the industry, I was a total hippie. I was making my own clothes, and they were terrible. Absolutely falling apart. It was what it was. I think part of the change comes from not having as much time as you did when you were younger. But it’s also about simplicity and comfort. I know that if I’m going to be on the floor pinning something but also have to give a presentation, there are certain pieces that work for both. I can be comfortable and still feel appropriately dressed. I’ve found a handful of things that work for me, and I don’t have to think too much about them anymore. A crisp, oversize white cotton button-down is half my closet. I also love a soft skirt and a big chunky sweater. Those are the pieces I always go back to and interchange depending on the day. I even wear sweaters in the summer. It’s cold in the Navy Yard!

Our pants fit incredibly well. The moment somebody gets into our pants, they’ll love us forever. Then it’s the jacket and the white shirt. Those three things are our gateway pieces, and everything else follows. You need a good blazer and a good leather piece, too. Once you find those foundational pieces that really work for you, everything else becomes easier.

I really want to see more women in our clothes. It’s not just about growing the business. It makes me happy when I see people put on our clothes and feel really good. We work so hard to make sure things fit the way a woman wants them to fit. I just want more people to enjoy that.

This interview has been edited and condensed for clarity.

#6857. The Rise of the Maximum Visibility Bra

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The summer of 2014, I was a college student working at a small-town lingerie store that lined its walls exclusively with neutrals. Every day, I sold women flesh-toned bras—black if they were really feeling kinky—all while filling my own intimates drawer with the same styles. Undressing in front of a guy that same summer, he voiced genuine disbelief over my nude, industrial-strength strapless bra. He wasn’t so much confused over its gravity-defying function as the fact that it was there, at all.

The early 2010s set a nearly impossible goal for young women: naturally perky breasts without any apparent scaffolding. But that’s all changing. Pop princess Adela’s exposed bras have become as central to her identity as her electric pink hair. Once left to jiggle freely beneath semisheer cotton, Charli XCX’s chest is now more often displayed in intricate lingerie. Several months back, Saint Laurent’s Spring 2027 front row was littered with boudoir-inspired looks while Prada and Fendi literally sent bras-as-tops down the runway. Bras have come out of hiding.

Somewhere between the “free the nipple” movement in 2012 and the impending #MeToo revolution of 2017, boobs became less of a sexualized spectacle and more of a political battleground. Victoria’s Secret canceled its fashion show as basics-driven intimates brands like Cuup and Arq gained traction for destigmatizing natural shapes. Then, breasts bounced back into cultural focus. The shift might be credited to the climate—conservatism generally propels women to take shelter in traditional femininity, or it is thrust upon them—as well as the twin forces of Sydney Sweeney and semaglutide weight loss. Either way, women got thinner while their chests grew larger—reflecting a beauty standard we hadn’t seen in a generation. Implants entered a new era of demand and dialogue, as celebrities like Kylie Jenner and Megan Fox transparently joined the big-breasted brigade.

But boobs aren’t just bigger—they’re bolder. Gone are the days of basics and bralettes. It’s all about va-va-voom underwire, satin, and lace—even push-ups have returned to the conversation. Still, “there’s a difference between showing your bra and showing off your bra,” discerns Tara Ocello, director of brand marketing at intimates brand Skarlett Blue.

Last month, the recently revived Victoria’s Secret Fashion Show broadcast Jenner strutting down the runway, her 445-cc-moderate-profile silicones outfitted in a record-breaking $50 million bra. Bella Hadid, Candice Swanepoel, and Adriana Lima showed off the iconic mall brand’s finest embroidered balconettes, lacy push-ups, and satin plunge bras. Angels aside, the reintroduction of these runway collections is also indicative of a shift in consumer product demand—a spokesperson for Victoria’s Secret claims there’s “strong interest” in fashion-forward details like lace, satin, logo hardware, and the brand’s statement shine straps. The Very Sexy Push-Up Bra remains the number one of the three mainline bras in its “Very Sexy” category. “Today’s customer wants a bra that performs beautifully and deserves to be seen,” says a Victoria’s Secret spokesperson. “It’s expanded how customers engage with [our bras] as both foundation pieces and intentional styling elements.”

The luxury London retailer Agent Provocateur has been positioning its designs as statement pieces since it launched in the mid-’90s—the last time exposed bras enjoyed prevalence. Creative director Sarah Shotton noticed the trend bubbling several years back on the Glastonbury grounds and immediately returned to the design studio. “We predicted this and designed [for it] about a year and a half in advance. I am constantly thinking, could it be worn as an outerwear bra?” she says. “I think if customers are going to invest in it, they want it to work harder than just in the bedroom. Lingerie as outerwear is just getting bigger and bigger.”

Category transcension is, of course, good for business. Victoria’s Secret reported sales up 10 percent in its second quarter, and Agent Provocateur has also seen revenue growth this year. Eager to capitalize on the movement, contemporary brands like Lioness are now engineering built-in underwear to imitate the effect of an exposed bra. This week, Charlize Theron wore a similar style by Tom Ford to the Governors Awards.

I have, undoubtedly, been influenced. This year, I have spent more money at Victoria’s Secret than cumulatively on lingerie in the past decade—acquiring my own Very Sexy Push-Up, and a turquoise polka-dot number. These are bras I’ve never gravitated toward, let alone purchased, because they seemed like impractical additions to my wardrobe. I’ve had to ask myself: Can you wear leopard-print under a white tee? What if the lace breaches my neckline?

Somewhere between Erin Brockovich and Blake Lively’s character in The Town, an exposed bra—particularly a colorful one—became a media shorthand for unsophistication, and more often than not, sexual availability. In simpler terms: tacky. Now, tacky is trending. Along with diamanté-encrusted snapbacks and sky-high peep-toes, straps in dynamic hues are stretched across the décolleté of It girls en masse. Printed cups are peeking out of the necklines. Some fashionistas are forgoing the shirt altogether. “There's a real cultural shift from polished perfection toward something more expressive, unpredictable, and a little undone,” claims Ocello. Or, perhaps a little more human.

This certainly isn’t a trend for everyone. According to Shotton, Agent Provocateur’s biggest sellers are corsets—a style that was once also considered strictly lingerie, but has since sashayed into the light. One wonders if their popularity is a gateway to full bra-baring, especially as GLP drugs become more readily available, and women more comfortable in their bodies. But with a Blue Wave washing in Woke 3.0, how long do boobs—and their bold accessories—have left? In all seriousness, might we look back on this moment with the same disdain we once reflected on early-aughts raunch culture? Only social media will tell. In the meantime, I plan for my collection to keep growing.

#6851. Hailey Bieber Brings New Life to an Archival Carolyn Bessette Kennedy Look

It was pulled from the Yohji Yamamoto Spring/Summer 1998 collection

As one of the leaders of modern-day minimalism, it makes sense that Hailey Bieber would wink to the woman who arguably started it all: Carolyn Bessette Kennedy.

This afternoon, Mrs. Bieber attended the 2026 DealBook Summit in Lincoln Center—a conference that unites some of the biggest leaders in tech, business, and politics—to discuss her mega-successful beauty brand, Rhode.

At the summit, she pulled out a supreme fashion reference, with an archival set from the Yohji Yamamoto Spring/Summer 1998 collection, once worn by none other than serious Yamamoto enthusiast CBK.

The finely tailored look consisted of a black Mandarin-collar jacket with a pinched waist and a split hem, plus a row of matching buttons dotting the front.

It was paired with a cream linen midi skirt that hugged Bieber’s legs, and for accessories, she matched the jacket with inky patent-leather pumps and one of CBK’s favorite finishing touches: an acetate headband.

Bessette Kennedy famously wore this look back in June 1998, when she attended the Robin Hood Foundation benefit with her husband, John F. Kennedy Jr., at the 26th Street Armory in New York City. For the occasion, she tied her hair back into a ponytail and went sans jewelry.

We were all reminded of CBK’s chic style earlier this year, when FX released the smash Love Story: John F. Kennedy Jr. and Carolyn Bessette miniseries. Stars like Bella Hadid and Sarah Pidgeon (the latter of whom portrayed Bessette Kennedy in the show) all seemed to take a page out of the former Calvin Klein publicist’s styling book for their own outfits. And Bieber also joined in on the fun.

But while many have tried to recreate the late muse’s look, few can really capture the true essence of CBK. Clearly, Bieber is one of them.

#6848. The Property Brothers Reveal Why Lighting Tops Their Holiday Chore Checklist

The holidays are upon us, and while we busy our hands with turkeys, trees, and tinsel, the Property Brothers are gifting fans a few friendly tips for hosting across generations. Partnering with Home Instead, Drew and Jonathan Scott have developed a holiday chore checklist that every host should complete before guests arrive.

“The holidays are all about bringing family together. But when you're hosting aging loved ones, a little preparation can go a long way in making sure everyone feels comfortable and safe. As home renovation experts, we know that it's often the smallest changes that make the biggest difference,” the brothers explain.

Ahead, we’re sharing their top tips for a safe, festive, and inviting home this holiday season.

While string lights and extension cords are all holiday decor staples, they can be a major tripping hazard. To prevent spending the holidays in the emergency room, the brothers recommend double-checking that loose ends and loops are neatly tucked away to keep paths and walkways clear throughout your home.

Slippery rugs, mats, and runners are another major safety hazard. Drew and Jonathan recommend reinforcing any pieces that could potentially slide with gripping tabs or replacing them with options that have better traction—especially in bathrooms where wet floors pose a greater risk.

While decorating with Christmas lights is at the top of everyone’s to-do list, your home needs more than a well-lit tree. Dark halls and stairways are major safety concerns, especially when hosting guests unfamiliar with your floor plan. Plug-in nightlights are a great option for improved visibility, and they can be decorative too.

A clear entryway for the holidays is a nonnegotiable. Outside the home, leaves and debris lining your front steps are as dangerous as they are unsightly. Before your guests ring the doorbell, take the time to rake, sweep, and clean as much as you can to ensure all steps, dips, and curbs are visible.

Drew and Jonathan clock in at well over six feet tall, but their guests may not be as gifted with height. If you’re used to storing things up high, the brothers recommend moving essentials like plates and glasses from high shelves and into lower, more easily accessible territory. Not only does this minimize straining, but it also reduces the risk of dropping pieces that could leave dangerous, messy shards in their wake.

#6839. The Only Apple Deals Worth Your Dollar This Black Friday

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

AirPods, MacBooks, and Watches for up to 28% off—this year’s sale is hot.

If it feels like life is one long string of sales—most of them being mediocre—you’re not wrong. Despite the near-constant deluge of Prime Days, anniversary events, and the like, some sales do legitimately stand above the rest. You know the one: Black Friday. It tends to bring some of the best deals of the year, including record-low prices on Apple tech.

Apple is notorious for not marking down its own products—on the contrary, the tech giant actually raised prices on Macs and iPads earlier this year—so we turn to retailers like Amazon when looking to save on everything from AirPods to Apple Watches. This year, Amazon’s Black Friday deals span 12 days (the sale is called Turkey 12 if you’d like to know too much), which means plenty of Apple discounts are already live. In the event that you’ve spilled coffee on your MacBook or lost one of your AirPods, you needn’t wait until Black Friday proper to shop.

Sure, there’s always the chance these deals could slightly deepen, but we’d wager we’re only looking at marginal price cuts at this point. Consider this: Shop now, shave a couple hundred bucks off of those tech upgrades you’ve been avoiding, then spend your holiday weekend watching football and reheating Thanksgiving leftovers instead of scrolling through Amazon. Your move.

AirPods reliably go on deep discount during Amazon sale events, and Black Friday has proven no different. The third-generation AirPods Pro have dropped to just $179, only $10 shy of their record-low price.

Although the new AirPods 5 aren’t marked down right now, they start at just $129, which is still lower than the discounted Pros. We’re hoping that these will go on sale for the first time on Black Friday. For those who prefer over-ear headphones, the AirPods Max 2 are $120 off (not quite their lowest price, but nothing to scoff at). You can also save on Beats wireless headphones and earbuds, including $150 off the ultra-popular Beats Studio Pro, which have Apple’s chip under the hood.

No Apple product experienced higher price hikes than laptops. The MacBook Air jumped $200 to $1,299 and the MacBook Pro climbed $400 to $1,999, driven by rising RAM and storage costs. To ease the pain, both the 13- and 15-inch MacBook Air models are up to 19% off. We haven’t seen deals this deep on Macs in quite some time.

If you prefer the Pro, those aren’t quite as discounted, but you can still shave up to $250 off of a souped-up model. Plus, Apple’s most affordable laptop, the MacBook Neo, just went on sale for the first time. Sure, it’s only a $50 discount, but it’s still the cheapest model by far—and fits the bill for most basic needs like watching Netflix and renewing your lease.

Although Apple Watches haven’t fallen victim to price increases this year, deals have been few and far between, which means these Black Friday discounts are liable to sell out fast.

The brand-new Apple Watch Series 12—which ushered in Apple’s first stab at readiness scores—is $70 off, dropping this to its lowest price ever. If you’re looking for a budget-friendly Apple Watch, opt for the SE 3, which has dipped to under $200—a verifiable steal given Apple’s current pricing dynamics. While the rugged Apple Watch Ultra 4 isn’t on sale right now, we’ll keep you posted should any surprise sales arise.

Apple Watch Series 12 (GPS+ Cellular, 42mm)

Back in June, Apple bumped the price of its entire iPad range by up to $200, which makes scoring one on sale all the more crucial. As predicted, previous-gen iPad Minis (an editor favorite) are $100 off after the revamped A20 Pro model was released earlier this month. Should you prefer a full-sized tablet, entry-level iPads, iPad Airs, and iPad Pros are all on sale, so it’s mostly a matter of use case in determining which to buy. If you’re going to watch TV on it while traveling, opt for the $399 model, while the Pro is better suited for getting creative work done.

Accessories are reliably among the easiest Apple products to save on during major sales. If you want to stick with Apple-branded gear, the second-generation AirTag, complete with improved Precision Finding, is sitting at a record-low $23, in addition to discounts on Apple Pencils, Magic Keyboards, and the like. You can also save on plenty of third-party power banks, wall adapters, and wireless chargers, which tend to see steeper discounts than Apple’s own gear.

#6835. Designers Reveal the Fastest Ways They've Upgraded a Kitchen Under $100

No renovation needed for these designer-approved swaps.

As you whip up a weeknight meal or prep for a dinner party, you might find yourself daydreaming—and envisioning the kitchen you wish you had. While your current cook space might be perfectly functional, it just doesn’t have the aesthetics you long for.

Alas, the kitchen is widely regarded as the most expensive room to renovate. As Houzz reports, the average cost to remodel a kitchen is currently $26,500. But if you’re happy with things like cabinets and appliances (or a renovation is far off in the future), and you want to focus on just the visuals, you can give the space a glow-up with as little as $100.

It can indeed be done, and ahead, designers are sharing their best budget-friendly upgrades.

Rugs are such thrift-able items. A good rug is basically a necessity in a kitchen, and it can easily be swapped with a new one that refreshes the visuals.

Brooke Mennen-Talsma, principal interior designer at Brooke MT Interiors, shares that in the above kitchen she designed, she added a thrifted runner that she discovered for just $40, adding a focal point to an arched pantry.

She says to not underestimate the power of secondhand, and a lot of her favorite pieces in her portfolio are thrifted items.

Switching in fresh hardware for your cabinets is something that can be done in an afternoon, and it can be gentle on the wallet, too. You can also choose to add backplates to your existing hardware, as Sherrell Neal of Sherrell Design Studio recommends. She calls it a “quick upgrade that can stay under $100,” depending on the size and number of cabinets.

While new lighting in a kitchen can quickly add up, you can add light in other, more economical ways.

For instance, as Kira Young, founder and principal designer at High Tide Design, says, you can simply go with a “cute countertop lamp with a pretty little shade.” She advises specifically purchasing a rechargeable lamp, with no messy cords and no need for an outlet. It can also be easily moved anywhere.

Homeowners are increasingly opting for collected, and even pleasantly cluttered, looks in their homes. But if you really want your kitchen visuals to look polished and pleasing, Heather Kirk, principal interior designer at Kirk Riley Design, suggests highlighting a “hero piece,” or a favorite item that shines and steals focus.

Think strong statement pieces that offer both beauty and function, “like a cheerful tea kettle on the stove or a dramatic footed bowl on the counter,” as Kirk recommends.

Emily Spanos, creative director of Emily June Designs, says that in one kitchen she designed, she painted only the lower cabinets a bright turquoise and kept the walls and upper cabinets a soft white. Paint is one of the cheapest ways to quickly upgrade a home, and it’s something that can effectively be used in the kitchen.

“Painting one element costs a fraction of painting everything, but still highlights what you want the eye to fall on,” Spanos says. “A gallon of paint and a weekend’s work keeps you under budget.”

In addition to going with lower cabinets, you could consider painting an island, pantry door, the trim, or the inside of a glass-front cabinet.

To allow your eye to have multiple things to admire in your kitchen, go with a “layered look,” as designers call it. Think: linens, decor, rugs, and artwork.

Spanos says that she has accomplished this look for under $100 in a few projects she’s worked on. In one art-filled home, she carried the creative feel into the kitchen by displaying art on a couple shelves above the sink area. In another kitchen, she laid down a black-and-white geometric rug for visual interest, which tied into the matte black hardware.

She says, “A few quality tea towels, a small rug or runner, and a piece of art can all be found affordably, especially if you shop vintage or, even better, your own home.”

#6834. What to Eat for Healthier Skin and Slower Aging

A dietitian breaks down the foods, nutrients, and habits that may support skin from the inside out.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

On the quest to age smarter and slower, there’s a lot that your skin care can do. But perhaps one of the greatest contributions lies in the foods you consistently eat. Of course, diet and exercise are the pillars of good health, proper body function, and longevity, but a recent study done by scientists at the German Center for Neurodegenerative Diseases (DZNE) also reveals that 10 specific diets are linked to slower aging. Experts unpack the findings for ELLE and break down which foods you should incorporate or ditch from your diet to help improve skin health.

To understand the connection between diet and skin health, Caitlin Hosmer, MS, RD, LDN, a registered dietitian and co-founder of MenoPantry, says it’s important to define the notion of slowing down aging. She points out that this particular DZNE study didn’t look at wrinkles or how old people feel. “It looked at biological aging, measured with epigenetic clocks. These read chemical marks on our DNA (methylation) that shift as our cells age. In about 7,500 adults, the researchers found that following any of 10 healthy eating patterns more closely was associated with slower biological aging,” Hosmer explains. The 10 eating patterns or diets included in the study results are Mediterranean, Nordic, DASH, MIND, and plant-based. Hosmer says that while the effect was modest, this kind of study shows an association, not causation—proof that diet is the reason for the positive outcome—so it is still a meaningful signal.

Additionally, Mark Gilbert, nutritionist from The 1:1 Diet by Cambridge Weight Plan, tells ELLE that no single food has been satisfactorily studied and shown to be likely, on its own, to slow aging. “Of all the predictors of a long, healthy life, two of the best, which can be improved with diet, are muscle mass and strength, but this leads to a controversial conclusion,” says Gilbert. “The best nutritional intervention to increase muscle and strength is eating a high-protein diet. However, many scientists argue that high-protein diets have the opposite effect on longevity. So, if we can’t even agree on that, you can see that science has a long way to go to try and figure this out.”

One stat Hosmer found particularly striking was that over 70 percent of the biological pathways affected were the same across all ten diets. “I interpret that to mean that no single superfood is doing the work, and it affirms that [a combination of] food, diet, and nutrition impacts us on the cellular level. The foods these patterns share are what matter,” says Hosmer, who, along with Gilbert, breaks down the standouts:

Alongside individual foods, Hosmer says certain diet components have the greatest impact on aging. For starters, protein is a must. “As we age, our muscles respond less efficiently to the protein we eat. This is called anabolic resistance, and in women, the drop in estrogen makes it worse,” she explains. Building and retaining muscle isn’t only about strength; Hosmer says it’s our largest site for handling blood sugar and a strong predictor of independence later in life. “Many women eat less protein just when they need more.”

Gilbert adds that while protein is an important part of a balanced, longevity diet, there are some considerations to keep in mind before adding it to your meal. “I believe that because muscle and strength are so strongly associated with longevity and that protein, particularly later in life, supports them,” he says. The caveat, however, is that there is a potential mechanism (which has to do with the amino acid methionine) whereby too much protein could have negative effects. “Many scientists argue that, because in insects and some other animals, less protein along with calorie restriction increases lifespan, humans would similarly benefit. Also, some cite the so-called ‘Blue Zones’, alleged to show that plant-based, low-protein diets are superior. In my opinion, all of these have been shown to be invalid,” says Gilbert.

Fiber is another essential nutrient that you’ll reap from plant foods and mushrooms. Meat, fish, dairy, and eggs contain no fiber, and because our enzymes can’t break them down, Hosmer says that fiber travels intact to the large intestine to assist. Once there, fermentable fibers become food for the gut microbiome; other fibers mainly add bulk and keep things moving. “As gut bacteria ferment fiber, they make short-chain fatty acids, which nourish the cells lining the gut, help keep that barrier strong, and keep inflammation in check. Fiber also steadies blood sugar and helps the body excrete estrogen the liver has already processed, supporting healthy hormone balance. Yet more than 90 percent of American women don’t reach the recommended 21 to 25 grams a day,” Hosmer explains.

Plant diversity matters, too, and not just quantity. Hosmer points to research from the American Gut Project that found people who ate 30 or more different kinds of plant foods a week had more diverse gut microbiomes than those who ate 10 or fewer. “That’s because each plant brings its own mix of fibers and polyphenols, and some bacteria have preferences, so a varied diet feeds a broader community of microbes. A broader community does more jobs. It makes short-chain fatty acids that protect the gut lining, helps regulate immunity and inflammation, turns plant compounds into forms the body can use, and even helps process estrogen.” The variety of plants Hosmer adds seems to matter more than any single “perfect” plant.

You’ve likely heard of healthy fats, meaning omega-3s from fish and the polyphenol-rich fat in olive oil. Adding these to your diet can support cell membranes and help settle inflammation.

Also on the list for Gilbert is vitamin D, which he says studies have shown to be inversely related to the risk of mortality. “This does not prove that low vitamin D is the cause, but it is compelling evidence. Although some results are controversial, low [vitamin D] levels have been linked to all-cause, cardiovascular, cancer, and infection-related mortality,” he adds. Vitamin D is intimately involved in immunity, too. It protects T-cell immune response.

Although Gilbert says, “The only thing all good nutritionists agree on is that there is no one ‘best’ diet or ‘best’ food,” he warns that for most people, minimizing the intake of sugar and refined, starchy grains and increasing protein intake is, in my opinion and experience, the best approach, but, again, different people can thrive on different diets. “As in the examples I gave, many of these foods contain phytochemicals—plant chemicals like polyphenols, flavanols, cyanidins, and others – which affect disease risk or support systems in the body that are likely to serve you well in old age.”

Hosmer notes several other red flags as contributing to unhealthy skin: Diets dominated by refined carbohydrates and added sugars, heavily processed meat, and regular alcohol. “Each of these drives the chronic, low-grade inflammation researchers call “inflammaging,” and blood-sugar instability.”

Having seen so many gaps in these important nutrients in her practice, and understanding how women’s bodies change in midlife, led Hosmer and her team to build Menomix at MenoPantry. “One serving adds meaningful protein along with ground flaxseed and psyllium fiber, plus a range of plant ingredients. It’s made to help close the protein, fiber, and plant-variety gaps that so many women are carrying,” she adds.

To dive deeper into some of those gaps or bad habits that can send skin aging off the rails, Hosmer offers some eating patterns that push biology in the wrong direction:

The information can be overwhelming, but instead of purging everything in your fridge, Hosmer suggests first focusing on adding good foods instead of restricting less healthful options. “In my experience, that’s more sustainable, both because it feels less like deprivation and because the “good” foods can displace the ones that speed aging up instead of slowing it down. You don’t need to pick the “right” diet and follow it perfectly.”

She highlights that the DZNE study found few people scored well on all ten diets at once, yet better adherence to any of them helped. What matters is the common ground. Mediterranean, Nordic, DASH, MIND, and plant-based diets vary on the plate, but they have a common core: more fruits, vegetables, and whole grains, and less red and processed meat, and added sugar. “That shared core is where I’d start,” says Hosmer, offering this meal planning guide:

Increase your familiarity with, and use of, plant-based proteins. Plant-based proteins offer an excellent package of beneficial nutrients, including fiber (not found in animal products). If you aren’t already familiar, learn how to include whole soy foods (tofu, tempeh, edamame) in your diet; add beans and peas and lentils to dishes here and there. Nuts and seeds and nut and seed butters count here also.

Build meals around vegetables and fruit. Fill half your plate with them, and choose deep colors like leafy greens, berries, beets, and cabbage-family vegetables. Those colors come from polyphenols, plant compounds that act as antioxidants and feed beneficial gut bacteria.

Make your grains whole and varied. Oats, rye, barley, brown rice, and whole-wheat bread are staples of the Nordic and DASH patterns, the two most consistently linked to slower aging in this study. Try a grain you haven’t eaten before—quinoa? Farro? Millet or teff? They are all cooked the same way—in boiling water, just with different grain-to-water ratios. Note: raise fiber gradually, with plenty of water, so your gut has time to adjust.

Count your plants, not your calories. Aim toward 30 different kinds of plant foods a week. Veggies, fruits, nuts, seeds, whole grains, beans, peas, herbs, and spices all count. A homemade trail mix, an interesting salad, or a smoothie with a handful of fruit and veggies can add three or four at once.

Choose your fats deliberately. Most of these patterns favor unsaturated fats over saturated ones. Use extra-virgin olive oil as your everyday fat, and eat oily fish two or three times a week.

Keep red and processed meat occasional, and keep added sugar low. Every one of these diets limits them.

Quality matters. Note that “plant-based” isn’t automatically healthy. A version of the plant-based diet built on refined grains, sweets, and sugary drinks tended to be associated with faster aging, as did a pro-inflammatory eating pattern overall. Food quality matters, not just food category.

Diet is the foundation the other efforts rest on. Collagen supplements and skin care can help, but they can’t make up for a body that lacks what it needs to build and protect tissue. According to Gilbert, collagen supplements contain very high levels of the amino acid glycine, which he says can balance the potential harms of proteins that are high in methionine.

Hosmer further explains that collagen peptides break down during digestion into amino acids and small peptides. Your body then decides where to use them. “To actually build collagen, it needs enough total protein, plus vitamin C. Vitamin C is a required helper for the enzymes that stabilize the collagen structure. It also needs minerals like zinc and copper. So a collagen supplement taken on top of a protein-poor, micronutrient-poor diet is not going to be optimized,” Hosmer says. “Collagen is also an incomplete protein: it’s low in leucine and lacks tryptophan entirely. It can add to your protein intake, but it isn’t a substitute for complete proteins when it comes to muscle.”

On the surface, what we eat affects the skin from the inside. Hosmer notes that polyphenol-rich foods and healthy fats support the skin’s own defenses against oxidative stress, and high blood sugar leads to glycation, which stiffens collagen fibers. The gut matters here, too. “Researchers call the connection the gut–skin axis. When the gut lining is healthy, and the microbiome is well fed, inflammation throughout the body tends to stay lower, and the skin reflects that. External products can only do so much if we aren’t supporting our skin from the inside,” Hosmer says.

Diet plays a major role in overall skin health and slower aging, but Hosmer argues that it’s not the full picture. To optimize more youthful-looking skin, consider other factors that may be equally as important as diet.

#6827. A New Exhibition Celebrates the Mantilla in Fashion and Art

The garment finally gets its due credit at The Hispanic Society Museum & Library.

Few garments have been as imitated, reinterpreted, and misunderstood as the mantilla. The iconic lace veil with Spanish roots has endured for centuries. What started as an object traditionally worn for ceremonies has had a renaissance via Madonna, Rosalía, and Dolce & Gabbana, just to name a few. That’s just one of the reasons why The Hispanic Society Museum & Library in New York is opening its new exhibition “Mantilla: Fashion Unveiled” beginning November 20. The museum calls the mantilla the “most distinctive garment in Spanish women’s fashion with origins dating to the 16th century.” Curated by Spanish-born scholar Carlos Usabiaga López, the show merges fashion, art, and archival material to shine a light on an iconic garment that has been donned on screen, on runways, and on red carpets for generations.

“The understanding of the mantilla in all its complexity is often limited, either by a superficial reading of the symbol itself or by its association with an old-fashioned, rather conservative image of Spain,” López tells ELLE. “This exhibition challenges the perception of the mantilla as an anachronistic relic of the past and reclaims its continued cultural relevance and subversive potential. From Spanish countercultural movements such as ‘La Movida Madrileña’ to drag performance, the mantilla has repeatedly been appropriated and reimagined in ways that reveal its capacity to carry new meanings.”

The exhibition is the second installment in the Hispanic Society’s multiyear fashion initiative, which focuses on reinterpreting its own renowned collection through a new lens. The museum was founded in 1903 by American philanthropist Archer Milton Huntington and has many visual examples of the garment, including Francisco Goya’s famed portrait of María del Pilar Teresa Cayetana de Silva y Álvarez de Toledo, the 13th Duchess of Alba (1762–1802). “By bringing together works of art, fashion, and material culture, the iconography of the mantilla activates a collection of such an extraordinary diversity, through unexpected dialogues between the museum’s different departments and international loans,” adds López.

The new exhibition pulls from many cultural moments new and old, including the “Spanish Craze,” which was a period of cultural fascination with Spain in the United States spanning 1890 to 1930. “During this period, the mantilla became part of a highly recognizable visual language through which Spain was imagined and represented in America,” says López. “Spanish performers who travelled to the United States achieved remarkable success: Raquel Meller, for instance, appeared on the cover of Time magazine in 1926, while Concha Piquer starred in From Far Seville (1923).” He also points to the reinterpretation of the mantilla in The Devil Is a Woman (1935), starring Marlene Dietrich.

Fashion naturally plays a role in the show, especially through the imagination of emerging designers, like Arturo Obegero. “Throughout the history of fashion, major designers such as Cristóbal Balenciaga and Óscar de la Renta have drawn inspiration from this [Spanish] visual language, including the mantilla,” adds López. “However, it was a curatorial decision to bring contemporary designers, including emerging voices, into direct conversation with the masterpieces in order to make justice to the mantilla’s enduring influence on fashion to this day.”

The mantilla has been loved (and reinvented) in contemporary fashion at least since the 1940s, when it appeared in numerous fashion magazines. Today, the references are endless and people of all cultures and backgrounds are embracing it. “I think there is something particularly beautiful about the fact that those who have historically been excluded from the dominant narrative, who may have felt unrepresented or even oppressed by these national symbols, can now feel encouraged to revisit this folklore and expand its boundaries to make room for others to do the same,” says López. As the exhibition makes clear, the mantilla’s compelling story is still unfolding.

“Mantilla: Fashion Unveiled” is on view at The Hispanic Society Museum & Library from Nov. 20, 2026, through March 28, 2027.

#6822. Before & After: Two Best Friends Turned a Stripped-Down Tudor Into a Colorful, Antique-Filled Dream Home

Designer Anne Hulcher Tollett understood her client’s signature aesthetic better than anyone.

Anne Hulcher Tollett can’t remember a life without her best friend, Van. They met in a playpen as toddlers and quickly became an integral part of each other's lives, with many childhood memories taking place in Van’s house growing up. Tollett has been with her best friend through every milestone, including as her maid of honor at her wedding to her husband, Sean, and when the couple put an offer on a suburbanized Tudor home in Richmond, Virginia.

The five-bedroom house had good bones and an intuitive flow, but it needed help getting some of its charm back. When Van and Sean bought it for their family of four (plus their dog, Jelly Bean, and a small flock of chickens), it was whitewashed and lacked architectural character. In short, it wasn’t up to par with Van’s eclectic style, according to Tollett, but as the principal designer of Hanover Avenue, she reassured her bestie that she could easily take it from suburban chic to warm, layered, and personality-filled without having to completely rework the interior.

“Van’s aesthetic is diametrically opposed to ‘simple’ or ‘builder-grade,’ and although this house had been meticulously renovated and maintained by the previous owners, some of the charm she loves had been removed,” says Tollett. The designer and her team focused on lighter renovations and cosmetic upgrades, walking her best friend through every decision, from wallpaper choices to reworking the staircase to highlight the amazing treasures Van has collected on her travels over the years.

Throughout the renovation, Tollett and Van leaned into their sisterly shorthand, resulting in a project with very few disruptions. “Our biggest challenge was creating unexpected quirky moments without taking down walls or undoing all of the thoughtful and well-built upgrades that came with the house,” Tollett explains. “We found places on tables, walls, and bookshelves to highlight everything Van and her family have collected over the years. It makes the house feel like theirs and not the product of some design firm.”

Walls were covered with grasscloth, patterns were layered on throw pillows and vintage rugs, and anything that wasn’t antique or pre-owned was chosen to add a touch of patina to the space, allowing Van’s collections to truly sing.

Once Tollett was finished with the renovation, the house told the story of their friendship and a life well lived. Because the designer knows Van like the back of her hand, she was able to create a retreat that feels like a true reflection of who she is and how her family lives without editing out any of Van’s eccentricities. “We understood her design sensibility and took something well-built but fairly plain and made it fun, pretty, and interesting at every turn, which is exactly how anyone would describe Van!” Tollett adds. Besides, who better to design your house than the person who knows you better than yourself?

FAST FACTSDesigner: Anne Hulcher Tollett of Hanover AvenueLocation: Richmond, VirginiaThe Space: A 4,600-square-foot Tudor home with five bedrooms, four full bathrooms, and two half-baths

The barrel back and turquoise chairs were finds that Tollett sourced for Van’s previous home, proving that timeless profiles and accessible sizes can work in many different spaces throughout their lifetimes.

“A room like this with a vaulted ceiling and white walls creates a wonderful backdrop for bold pops of color and patterns because it can visually hold its own against furnishings with personality,” she says. “Van had the beautiful sideboard, and we loved pairing it with the tattooed table.”

Possibly one of the most remarkable makeovers in the entire house, Tollett coated the entryway in deep blue paint, topping it off with darling wallpaper on the ceiling for added dimension. “This is a great example of how a can of paint, a roll of wallpaper, and some strategically placed treasures can transform a room,” she says.

Large-scale grasscloth wallpaper instantly gave the once-all-white box character and rhythm, setting a serene backdrop for the rest of the unique finds in this dining room. The chandelier hanging over the table had long been on Tollett’s list to use in a project, and Van’s home was the perfect spot.

“The dining room is right near the front door, so it’s hung to look like it's being blown by the entrance breeze—a true classic with a twist!” the designer says.

Above the antique bar (whose natural patina plays so well with the wallpaper), are two framed photos of puppets, which Van found during one of her globe-trotting trips. She carried them around in her backpack, and Tollett loved the story behind them so much that she had them framed. On the opposite wall, behind the piano, is a portrait of Van’s grandmother in a vibrant green dress.

The kitchen was well-maintained when Tollett got her hands on it, but it didn’t feel all that fun, and it needed more than simple cosmetic refreshes like other areas of the home.

“We removed the big-box island lighting and reworked the storage, hood, and casework on the range wall,” Tollett explains. “We did the same on the wall opposite the range,” she adds, which was in fantastic shape, so they kept it.

To give the cook space more character, the designer installed a custom Delftware tile backsplash. The island was also in good shape, so Tollett upgraded the hardware, faucet, face frames, and lighting above it, opting for three Pinch pendant lights which bring an interesting, natural texture to the space.

“Open concept kitchens are tricky because they are seen from every angle, so we knew we needed to improve the storage to hide clutter,” she adds. Opposite the range wall, Tollett removed the open shelves, creating closed casework instead and topping everything off with pretty hardware and colors that play well with the adjacent dining and living rooms.

Tollett knew she was going to wrap this powder room in a beautiful butterfly-and-bird wallpaper, so she custom-matched a paint to fully drench the room in a whimsical emerald green hue. They even had their wonderful wallpaper installers cut out a few extra birds and butterflies to extend the design onto the ceiling, creating a more immersive experience. “We wanted the powder room to pack an equal punch to the bold, blue entryway it connects to,” she explains.

One of the mantras Tollett and her team at Hanover Avenue follow is “wonderfully weird,” which the hand sculpture extending through the wall to hold the soap perfectly captures. “The space feels not only more fun but also much more personalized to our clients!”

During the renovation, Van’s mother passed away, so Tollett transformed this almost-forgotten area into an homage to the matriarch. “We reused her beautiful plate rack and hung all her flawless collections of plates on the walls,” the designer says, officially turning this room, which overlooks the vegetable gardens, into the “plate room.”

They papered the walls in warm neutral grasscloth and hung relaxed Roman shades to softly filter the natural light, furnishing the room with many of the fabulous pieces Van already owned (and loved). “This is a wonderful example of ‘reimagining what you have,’ and finding ways to remember the ones we love in our own rooms,” Tollett adds. “Van’s mom was the coolest!”

“Decades of sleepovers in Van’s childhood bedroom allowed me to execute exactly what she was hoping to achieve,” Tollett explains. Though there are many layers of pattern in this sitting area, the designer managed to prevent it from feeling busy by keeping the same serene color palette for most of the textiles, minus a few pops of red for a warm balance.

“This room is a grown-up translation of Van’s entire design sensibility that began when she was young and was nurtured in her adulthood during her travels,” Tollett adds.

On the other side of the room is a calming wash of warm white paint and natural textures, giving Van a visual place of rest ideal for sleeping. Tollett calls it a “well-collected, well-traveled, layered space that seems to have evolved all on its own—a sanctuary for Van and Sean,” which makes the designer especially happy since she was the maid of honor at the couple’s wedding.

The primary bath really only needed some cosmetic upgrades, but they did a lot of heavy lifting. Airy Roman shades soften the space while Van’s mother’s bench and one of her existing rugs give it personality. By opting for two warmer paint colors and swapping out key fixtures, such as the lights, hardware, and faucets, Tollett created a serene, sophisticated escape that looks completely new, all with the same bones.

Tollett tapped into her childhood memories with her best friend when designing this room. “During slumber parties, Van and I fell asleep across from each other in twin beds, so it felt fitting to create another bedroom that fosters the same kind of childhood chatter,” she says, adding that stepping inside is like stepping into a time machine going back to 1983.

Along with the beds, much of the look was directly inspired by Van’s childhood bedroom, the round green pillow on the bed having been plucked directly from it. The pre-existing Tudor windows were perfect for the vintage vibe.

Tucked away at the far end of the home is a spot connected to the sunroom lounge. Dubbed the “dayroom”—an informal spot dedicated to relaxation and reading—Tollett wanted it to feel cozy and as collected as the rest of the home but still a bit private. They washed the walls in a soft gray-green hue and did the same to the custom day bed, finishing off the room with antique bookcases in a complementary gray tone. It’s easy for small rooms like these to feel overcrowded, but the pale color palette takes away some visual weight.

As founder and principal designer of Hanover Avenue, Anne Hulcher Tollett is intimately involved in every project the Richmond-based design firm takes on, from high-end residential to boutique commercial. Tollett finds a thrill in uncovering her clients’ design styles and endless joy in creating their dream homes that reflect who they are.

#6818. Where Is Caroline Ellison, Portrayed by Julia Garner in The Altruists, Now?

The cryptocurrency executive spent 11 months in prison.

This story contains spoilers for The Altruists.

Netflix’s The Altruists tells the true story of FTX, the celebrated cryptocurrency exchange, and the trading firm Alameda Research, which both collapsed amid accusations of wrongdoing by executives. Anthony Boyle and Julia Garner play Sam Bankman-Fried, who founded the company, and Caroline Ellison, the co-CEO of Alameda, respectively. The two, who met while working for the New York City firm Jane Street, are said to have been a couple at one point. Both were sent to prison on charges of fraud, with Ellison having been released this year. Here’s what you need to know about where she is now.

A native of Massachusetts and a Stanford grad, Ellison joined Alameda as a trader in 2018. She was promoted to co-CEO in 2021.

Ellison was sentenced to 24 months in prison in 2024 after pleading guilty to seven charges, including wire fraud and money laundering. Prosecutors praised Ellison for her cooperation, which included testifying against Bankman-Fried. “In her many meetings with the government, Ellison approached her cooperation with remarkable candor, remorse and seriousness…and she persevered despite harsh media and public scrutiny and Bankman-Fried’s efforts to publicly weaponize her personal writings to discredit and intimidate her,” they wrote.

Ellison served about 11 months in a Connecticut prison before being moved to what was referred to as community confinement, which typically means being under house arrest or living in a halfway house. She was released in January 2026.

While in college, Ellison became interested in effective altruism. The movement, which is popular within tech circles, urges people to use evidence to make decisions that will yield the most good for society. That can mean choosing to take a high-paying salary rather than volunteering, believing that the money earned will provide more benefit.

The nonprofit Manifund, a grant-funding organization that was inspired by the effective altruism movement and had deep ties to FTX, hired Ellison. The nonprofit’s CEO, Austin Chen, announced in September that she had begun working over the summer. “I believe in redemption. Caroline has admitted her faults, worked to make creditors whole, and served her time in prison. I’d like to give her the opportunity to contribute back to the world; I hope that the world will concur,” he wrote.

Chen quoted Ellison in his announcement. “I’m deeply sorry for what I did, and I completely understand why people may not want to work with me. But I’m very grateful to Austin for giving me a second chance, and judging me on my current work performance rather than my past. I feel very lucky not only to have a job, but to be working somewhere that I think is doing great work and has a mission I believe in,” she wrote.

#6817. How a Connected Home Supports a Good Night’s Rest

How a wellness content creator uses Hubspace from The Home Depot to curate an environment for better sleep.

As a certified holistic health coach and wellness content creator, Carolina Salazar knows the importance of a peaceful wind-down routine before bed. She trusts Hubspace from The Home Depot that powers many of the smart devices in her home, to help ease her into a good night’s sleep.

Hubspace, The Home Depot’s exclusive smart home platform, powers more than 250 products across select private brands—like Hampton Bay, Husky, and Vissani—as well as national brand partners. It effortlessly connects everyday essentials, including smart security, indoor and outdoor lighting, appliances, switches, watering controls, and more.

Some of the Hubspace app’s most practical features help users personalize lighting and improve home security. Hubspace Connect, for example, lets you control compatible lighting from a single switch, while Vacation Mode is a security feature that turns lights and smart plugs on and off while you’re away. One of Salazar’s favorite features is Circadian Rhythm, which automatically transitions lights from cooler, whiter tones to warmer ones as the day goes on.

“I can set my lights to follow my circadian rhythm, so they shift automatically throughout the day,” says Salazar. “And when they start to turn warm in the evening, that’s my cue to slow it down.” That can mean cozying up with a book, brewing a cup of hot tea, or, in her case, lighting a candle at her bedside and journaling. When Salazar is finished putting her thoughts to paper, she uses the Hubspace app to adjust the color, temperature, and brightness of her lights, dimming them as she gets closer to sleep. This can even be programmed automatically in order to reduce screen time before bed.

“Colder rooms bring better sleep, so I also have Hubspace turn on my AC at night,” she says.

The Hubspace app is free to download and easily syncs with your home’s smart devices. You can enable voice control for screen-free commands—also a plus if you’re trying to reduce your pre-sleep screen time. And the app runs on both WiFi and Bluetooth—so even if the WiFi is down, your nighttime routine can still run seamlessly.

#6816. Jennifer Lawrence Breaks Her Silence on Viral Connor Wood Smoothie Comment—and Corrects a Specific Detail

Might need to slightly edit my new favorite vocal stim.

“I saw you on 8th Avenue drinking a smoothie, and you paid me no mind.” Jennifer Lawrence typed these words on her keyboard, and they have lived rent-free in Connor Wood’s mind ever since. The viral moment even landed the Dancing With the Stars finalist a Smoothie King collab and had him pulling up to rehearsals in this iconic tee:

But the internet’s new favorite vocal stim might need a slight edit since J. Law herself publicly addressed it for the first time—and made a very specific correction.

During a joint appearance with her Hunger Games co-star, Josh Hutcherson, on Watch What Happens Live!, Jen broke her silence on the hilarious domino effect of her initial comment when a fan asked if she’s seen all the memes and reactions online.

“I don’t know how to feel about it. I did not expect that to go viral. I don’t...I don’t really get it,” she admitted, before Josh jokingly chimed in, “I don’t know what any of those words mean.”

She then clarified that she had accidentally “swapped” the location where she actually spotted Connor.

“Yeah, it was also Greenwich,” she explained, later adding, “It was across from my old apartment.”

This entire saga kicked off when Connor admitted that Jen was his celebrity crush in a BuzzFeed Celeb interview earlier this month, saying, “I just want just a little acknowledgment from Jennifer Lawrence that I’m funny, and then that’s all I need.”

The Oscar winner then dropped her now-infamous comment under a clip of the interview, and the rest was history!

I don’t know about you, but “I saw you on Greenwich Avenue drinking a smoothie, and you paid me no mind” just doesn’t hit the same. But for the sake of being biblically accurate, I’ll lock in and remember that!

#6812. The Best Shearling Shoes for Cold-Weather Style and Comfort

Cozy, polished pairs to wear all winter long.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

There’s a reason shearling keeps coming back every cold season. It’s the rare material that manages to be cozy and stylish at once, with a plush, cloud-like lining that wraps your feet in warmth and a rich texture that instantly makes an outfit feel more intentional. What began as practical gear for sheepherders and pilots has become a fixture of modern closets, showing up on everything from chic boots to sleek slippers, ballet flats, and fashion-forward sneakers.

Part of its staying power is function. Shearling is naturally insulating, breathable, and moisture-wicking, so your feet stay warm without overheating. Versatility is another reason why shearling has become so popular. The same material that elevates a pair of jeans and a sweater on a weekend errand run can make lounging at home feel like a small luxury.

Whether you’re hunting for the perfect slip-on to kick off at the door, a boot to carry you through winter, or a statement piece that turns heads, below are the must-have shearling shoes that earn a spot in your rotation this season.

Worn with tailored trousers, a slouchy knit, or bare ankles and a long wool coat, the shearling ballet flat moves effortlessly between the polished and the pared-back. The easiest way to make winter feel a little more cozy and luxurious.

Le Monde Beryl Apres Ski shearling-lined suede ballet flats

The loafer, softened for winter. A shearling lining lends quiet warmth to a classic silhouette, proving that heritage tailoring and true comfort can share the same shoe.

#6811. The Best Gingerbread Perfumes to Shop for a Cozy, Elevated Winter Scent

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

These are the festive, spicy perfumes worth wearing now.

’Tis the season to embrace all things cozy, and that includes gingerbread. The scent isn’t just wafting from our ovens and favorite coffee shops anymore. It’s showing up in our everyday lives, too, as gingerbread fragrances grow more popular.

In perfume, gingerbread is a gourmand accord: warm ginger, molasses, and cinnamon, usually rounded out with vanilla or woods. Ginger is a clever ingredient. Perfumers often use it as a top note, where it adds sparkle and energy, and it pairs beautifully with pepper, citrus, rich woods, and amber. That’s why a good gingerbread perfume can feel cozy without turning into a candle.

Leave it to Squishmallows to create the cutest gingerbread fragrance, complete with a vintage-inspired Cam the Cat bottle. This cozy scent blends ginger, coffee biscuit, and caramel for a sweet, snuggly treat that smells like a warm bakery on a chilly morning.

Jo Malone’s Ginger Biscuit is back for the holiday season. This warm, spicy scent blends ginger, cinnamon, and roasted hazelnut, giving your fragrance wardrobe a cozy, bakery-fresh addition that’s perfect for chilly days.

For on-the-go delight, this travel-size spray delivers warm sweetness with a hint of citrus zest. Slip it into your bag for a quick spritz of holiday cheer whenever you need it.

A scent that’s sure to put you in a festive mood, Baked Gingerbread & Brown Sugar makes holiday spritzing feel extra sweet, cozy, and celebratory.

Although it’s not entirely inspired by gingerbread, this Dior Spice Blend fragrance feels luxuriously rich, with notes of fresh rum, black pepper, and a ginger accord.

Freshly made gingerbread dough comes to mind with every whiff of this unisex fragrance. Described as a golden embrace, the scent feels familiar yet unexpected. It’s heartwarming yet sensual, and it lingers on the skin long after the holidays are over.

#6808. These Evergreen Perfumes Are Perfect for the Holidays (And Won’t Make You Smell Like a Candle)

Turns out there are a million ways to wear pine, balsam, and juniper notes.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

As much as I can appreciate a good gourmand or fruity floral, I am a spicy, woody scent girl through and through. And when the wintertime hits, there’s nothing better than the fresh fragrance of coniferous evergreens: pine, spruce, juniper, cypress, cedar, hemlock... if a plant is spiky and verdant, I want to smell it. Until recently, these notes were something I associated with candles—lovely to burn while I was watching a movie by the fire, not for spritzing onto myself during the day.

But, in the past few years, a veritable sleigh-load of evergreen-forward perfumes have launched, and I’ve deeply enjoyed almost every one I’ve sniffed. So, in the spirit of the holiday season, I’ve chatted with some experts about why this particular subcategory of woody fragrance seems to be so popular now and what notes pair best with evergreen for a wearable scent, plus rounded up a few of our perfumes with notes like pine, fir, cypress, spruce, and juniper that are perfect for this most festive time of year.

This rise in evergreen notes aligns quite well with what’s going on in the rest of the perfume world. “When it comes to perfume, this year has been all about touching grass, getting back to nature and living IRL,” says Steven Gontarski, senior manager of brand and public relations at retailer Luckyscent and host of the It’s Just Perfume podcast. “These scents remind us that we're actually living beings with a relationship to the natural world and not just disembodied fingers scrolling on endless screens.”

While we saw nature-inspired perfumes that emulate the sun, juicy berries, even the stinkiest flower in the world launch this fall, so it make sense that the plants most associated with winter are getting their time in the scent spotlight right now. There’s also something inherently cozy about pine.

“Evergreen is what comfort smells like when it isn’t dessert,” says Jeniece Trizzino, vice president of innovation and physical product at fragrance companies Scentbird and Drift. Sweet gourmands have held on to the “most comforting scent category” for a while, but Trizzino agrees that people are seeking a connection to nature now.

“Old trees, forest floor, damp earth: These scents give people a way to reset,” she says.

Evergreen notes are fairly versatile, though they tend to find their way into scents that lean, for lack of a better word, unisex rather than what we think of as traditionally feminine fragrances.

Gontarski loves notes of evergreen paired with incense, which adds a smokiness to the scent. He’s a fan of bright citrus notes too, saying they create “a perfect counterpoint” to resinous notes like pine.

“The best pairings put evergreen somewhere real,” Trizzino says—in other words, these woodsy notes pair best with other scents you can find in nature. She loves them with herbs like mint, basil, or eucalyptus (a different type of evergreen, BTW) and damp notes like moss, wet stone, or ocean air. If you’re still seeking the warmth that’s so common with winter gourmands, Trizzino says evergreen also pairs well with amber, musk, tobacco, and whiskey notes.

Kicking off this list with what I feel comfortable calling the most-likely-to-please-everyone evergreen scent. Yes, the juniper and cedar make for an undoubtedly pine-y fragrance, but it’s paired with warm, creamy notes of cardamom, suede, bourbon, and vanilla that almost bring it into gourmand territory.

This year, Nest turned its best-selling Holiday candle into a perfume you can wear. I’ll admit, I was skeptical that it would turn out well, but the brand really nailed it: The scent is much juicier than the candle, thanks to grapefruit and cranberry, but still has that familiar balsam note.

If you like a juicier scent story, surprise! There’s even an evergreen fragrance for you. This one goes dual meaning on the name “pine,” with notes of pineapple and pine tree, plus some florals and citrus. The result is something that truly works year-round.

This scent smells way more expensive than its under-$50 price tag. Evergreen really leads the charge too: There’s both fir balsam and torched cedar, paired with patchouli and vanilla for a smoky, warm fragrance.

Come for the gorgeous bottle, stay for the invigorating blend of pine, bergamot, eucalyptus, and cypress. It’s not quite as good as a Mediterranean vacation, but it’ll do until I save up enough miles for another trip.

Both my partner and I love this savory gourmand scent. Coriander mixes with juniper for a bright, peppery opening, but it dries down to a musky, spicy, floral thanks to clove, clary sage, magnolia, and mace.

“My all-time favorite is Santal 33,” Trizzino says. “I like woods that are warm and a little creamy.” You’ve almost definitely smelled Santal 33 before, but you might not have realized it has an evergreen (cedar) at its heart.

This aquatic evergreen scent from trendy fragrance brand Noyz is similar in vibes to Santal 33, but a bit more subtle on the skin. It really does smell like the inside of a spa you’d be thrilled to visit on your shittiest of days.

This is a perfume that made me totally forget I’m not typically a fan of rose scents. D’Orsay pairs the flower with a cypress, vetiver, and patchouli base that all adds up to a fragrance that feels both familiar and brand new—like an acoustic cover of your favorite song from high school.

Another cult classic that’s packed with evergreen notes, Gypsy Water opens with juniper berries and bergamot, has pine needles and incense at its heart, then finishes off with a cozy amber-vanilla-sandalwood base.

Fresh, laundry day-inspired scents are another big trend right now. Indie brand Cayla Gray’s take is (as the name implies) quite floral, but there’s white cedar at the base that makes this fragrance smell like walking in the woods on a crisp winter morning.

Nightchild is one of Gontarski’s favorite examples of an evergreen scent. In it, “incense pairs beautifully with evergreen notes,” he says. “Perfumer Michael Nordstrand tells a story of moonlight penetrating a forest canopy with frankincense mimicking its silver light.”

Orphéon is a classic spicy evergreen scent that’s beloved by many already. Named for a Parisian jazz bar that was popular in the 1960s, the vibe is sexy, smoky, and a little bit mysterious. The note of ginger gives it a sweet-spicy touch that you won’t find in any of the other fragrances on this list.

This is a (literally) fresh take on evergreen, perfect for someone who’s seeking a scent that’s light and airy rather than dark and spicy. It’s packed full of familiar herbs; fir, balsam and cypress; plus fig and olive for a touch of savory gourmand goodness.

If you’re looking for a scent that combines both sea and forest, this unisex scent from Giardini di Toscana will suit you just fine. It’s a marine fragrance that blends a little bit of citrus, a little bit of cyprus, and some salty notes for a fragrance that smells like the color turquoise.

Another of Gontarski’s favorites, G.494 has lots of citrus. “Notes of bitter orange and mandarin create a perfect counterpoint to the balsamic resins and evergreen needles,” he says. “It’s clean and invigorating in an understated way.”

Kara McGrath is a writer and editor with over 17 years of experience covering the beauty industry, women’s health, and a wide range of other lifestyle and wellness topics. After most recently serving as content director at Allure (2019-2025) and executive editor at Bustle (2014-2019), she is now fully freelance. In addition to Cosmo, her writing has appeared in Allure, The New York Times, Marie Claire, Business of Fashion, Playboy, NH Magazine, and more. Kara lives in New Hampshire with her dog, four cats, and a rotating cast of foster animals. You can find more of her work at karamcgrath.com.
